L24: ## 1 Executive Summary
L25: ## 1.1. Product Introduction
L26: Naldemedine (proposed trade name Symproic) is a new molecular entity (NME) in the drug class of peripherally-acting opioid receptor antagonists. The proposed indication of naldemedine is the treatment of opioid-induced constipation (OIC) in adult patients with chronic non-cancer pain.
L27: The to-be-marketed formulation of naldemedine is a 0.2 mg unscored, film-coated, immediate-release tablet. The proposed dosing regimen of naldemedine is 0.2 mg once daily, administered orally without regard to food intake.
L28: Naldemedine is a derivative of the opioid antagonist, naltrexone. Naldemedine acts as an antagonist at the \mu-, \delta-, and \kappa-opioid receptors. The mechanism of action of naldemedine is the inhibition of \mu-opioid receptors in the enteric nervous system of the gastrointestinal tract. The molecular structure of naldemedine consists of the naltrexone molecule with an added side chain that increases its molecular weight and polar surface area with respect to the parent molecule. In addition, the naldemedine molecule is a substrate of the P-glycoprotein (P-gp) efflux transporter (see Figure 1). These properties decrease the ability of naldemedine to cross the blood-brain barrier. As a result, naldemedine should not antagonize the \mu-opioid receptors in the central nervous system responsible for opioid-mediated analgesia.
L29: Figure 1: Molecular Structure of Naldemedine
L30: Naltrexone

Naldemedine-specific side chain
L31: ## 1.2. Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L32: The effectiveness of naldemedine for treatment of OIC in adults with chronic, non-cancer pain was supported by substantial evidence from adequate and well-controlled trials in the proposed patient population interpretability of efficacy assessments. Efficacy data to support this indication were primarily obtained in 2 replicate randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel group clinical trials that were adequately powered for the pre-specified primary efficacy analysis. The primary endpoint was the difference in proportion of responders, using a clinically meaningful, pre-specified responder definition.
L33: Opioid analgesics are among the most commonly prescribed classes of medications, and opioid induced constipation (OIC) is the most frequently reported side effect with chronic use.[1] Unlike many other opioid-induced side effects, patients rarely develop a tolerance to the GI side effects of opioid therapy.[2] Therefore, with increasing opioid doses, OIC tends to steadily worsen despite laxative use, often leading to reduced quality of life. The GI effects of opioids are often dose-limiting, thus compromising pain control.[3] Nalmedine is an NME in the class of peripherally-acting mu-opioid receptor antagonists (PAMORA). Nalmedine and the other PAMORAs are derivatives of non-specific/global opioid antagonists to which their molecular structure has been modified to restrict passage across the blood brain barrier. As a result, these drugs act on the peripheral nervous system to address the underlying mechanisms of OIC by antagonizing the mu-opioid receptors in the gastrointestinal tract, without having the CNS pharmacologic activity of global opioid antagonists.
L34: The effectiveness of nalmedine to treat OIC in patients with chronic non-cancer pain is supported by data from adequate and well-controlled trials in adults on stable opioid regimens for ≥4 weeks. In 2 replicate, placebo-controlled trials, superiority of nalmedine (compared to placebo) was demonstrated by primary efficacy analyses, in which statistically significant differences in the proportion of treatment responders favoring the nalmedine group were achieved, using a pre-specified, clinically-meaningful responder definition.
L35: Gastrointestinal toxicity was the predominant type of adverse reaction observed in clinical trials, leading to treatment discontinuation in 3.2% of nalmedine-treated subjects in the pooled phase 3 safety population. Opioid withdrawal was observed in nalmedine clinical trials, occurring in ~1% of subjects and was always accompanied by GI symptoms. Although GI toxicity and, to a lesser extent, opioid withdrawal decrease tolerability of nalmedine, events were often self-limited and resolved quickly following withdrawal of treatment. Overall, serious and/or life-threatening adverse reactions were rare, and no new safety concerns were identified for this drug. This reviewer considers the degree of risk associated with this drug to be acceptable for treatment of a non-life-threatening condition. Clinical trial data were sufficient for a favorable benefit-risk assessment to support approval.
L36: ## Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
L37: ## 1.3 Benefit-Risk Assessment
L38: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of Condition	
Opioid analgesics are commonly prescribed for the management of moderate to severe pain and represent one of the most prescribed classes of medications worldwide. Reports estimate that more than 3% of American adults are undergoing opioid therapy for more than 3 months for chronic noncancer pain.[1] OIC is the most frequent opioid side effect, reported to affect approximately 40% of patients receiving opioids for non-malignant pain.[4]
Opioids exert multiple constipating effects on the gastrointestinal (GI) tract, including slowed motility and intestinal transit, reduced inhibition of water and electrolyte secretion into the gut lumen, increased fluid absorption from the intestine, and increased anal sphincter tone.[5] Unlike other opioid effects including analgesia, patients typically do not experience tolerance to the GI effects of opioids.[2] Therefore, progressive worsening of OIC tends to occur with continued opioid use, despite use of laxatives.[3] Symptoms of OIC often impacting quality of life and can lead serious complications, such as anorexia, fecal impaction, fecal incontinence, pseudo-obstruction, impaired drug absorption, and urine retention. [5]. Due to the lack of GI tolerance to opioids, OIC can be dose-limiting and lead to inadequate pain management.[3]
Chronic opioid use for non-cancer pain is an increasing problem in the US and worldwide. Because OIC is the most common adverse reaction and does not improve with ongoing use (i.e., development of tolerance), the burden of OIC is also increasing. Although OIC is rarely life-threatening, this condition is associated with reduced quality of life and increased patient morbidity.

Current Treatment Options	
Currently, 3 drugs are approved for the treatment of OIC in patients with chronic non-cancer pain: two peripherally-acting mu-opioid receptor antagonists (PAMORAS)—Relistor (methylnaltrexone bromide, available in both subcutaneous and oral formulations) and Movantik (naloxegol, available as an oral tablet)—and lubiprostone (Amitiza), a chloride channel activator that is also approved for the treatment of chronic idiopathic constipation.
Naldemedine is a member of the PAMORA drug class. There are currently 3 approved drugs in this class (Relistor, Movantik, and Entereg), 2 of which—Relistor and Movantik—are approved for treatment of OIC in patients with chronic non-cancer pain. These drugs target the underlying mechanism of OIC via
L39: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
constipation (CIC) and irritable bowel syndrome (IBS). In addition to these drugs approved for OIC, standard-of-care management of OIC includes laxatives, fiber supplementation, and lifestyle interventions, such as increased fluid intake and physical activity. Though not specifically indicated for OIC, laxatives of all classes (i.e., stool softeners, bulk-forming agents, stimulants, osmotic agents, saline solutions, and lubricants) are extensively used to treat OIC and mitigate the various effects of opioids on the gastrointestinal tract (see "Analysis of Condition"). However, with long-term administration of opioids and worsening OIC, effectiveness of laxatives may lessen over time.
antagonism of opioid receptors in the gastrointestinal tract.

Benefit	
Naldemedine is a derivative of the opioid antagonist naltraxone that binds with high affinity to mu-opioid receptors and inhibits receptor function. The molecular structure of nalmedine consists of the naltraxone molecule with an additional side chain that increases the molecular weight and the polar surface area with respect to the parent compound and reducing its ability to cross the blood-brain barrier (BBB). In addition, nalmedine is a substrate of the P-glycoprotein (P-gp) efflux transporter, another mechanism that restricts penetration of nalmedine into the CNS. By limiting drug distribution to peripheral tissues, these molecular properties enable nalmedine to reduce OIC, while limiting its interference with the CNS-mediated analgesic effects of opioids and decreasing the risk of opioid withdrawal.
The effectiveness of nalmedine for treatment of OIC in patients with chronic, non-cancer pain was demonstrated in adequate and well-controlled clinical trials. The eligibility criteria were considered appropriate to ensure a study population sufficiently representative of the target patient population and the interpretability of efficacy assessments. In addition, the primary endpoint was considered an appropriate measure of a clinically meaningful treatment benefit upon which to base efficacy assessments.

Evaluation of the effectiveness of nalmedine to treat OIC in adults with chronic non-cancer pain was based primarily on data from 2 replicate multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled clinical trials, each with a 12-week treatment and evaluation period. The database used
Additional evidence of nalmedine efficacy was provided by secondary endpoint data. Although the clinical significance of data for each individual secondary endpoint as a
L40: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
as the primary source for efficacy analyses included 1095 subjects with OIC based on pre-specified diagnostic criteria (refer to Section 6.1.1) and 24 weeks of opioid use with a stable dosage regimen, who were randomized 1:1 to receive either naldemedine 0.2 mg once daily (n=549) or matching placebo (n=546). The designated primary endpoint was the difference in proportion of responders between groups, using a responder definition designed to assess treatment effect independent of rescue laxative use (i.e., SBM events) and over the entire treatment period (refer to Section 6.1.1).
The proportion of responders was higher among naldemedine-treated patients than placebo-treated patients, achieving statistical significance for both trials (p=0.002 and <0.0001). Sensitivity analyses of primary endpoint data yielded comparable results, thus demonstrating the robustness of primary efficacy results.
Furthermore, statistically significant treatment differences were demonstrated for all 4 key pre-specified secondary endpoints in both pivotal trials, each comparing the mean change in weekly frequency from baseline for one of the following specified bowel movement events—(1) change in the frequency of SBMs per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the treatment period, (2) change in the frequency of SBMs per week from baseline to Week 1 of treatment, (3) change in the frequency of CSBMs per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the treatment period, and (4) change in the frequency of SBMs without straining per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the treatment period.
The naldemedine safety population consists of 1163 subjects with chronic, non-cancer pain who received \ge 1 dose of naldemedine in phase 3 clinical trials.
standalone indicator of treatment benefit could not be established, consistent results demonstrating statistical superiority of naldemedine vs. placebo for all 4 endpoints in both clinical trials was considered supportive of the primary efficacy data.
The safety profile was generally comparable to the known safety profile of other PAMORA
L41: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
trials, as well as a similar population of placebo-treated subjects (total 2328 subjects). The primary sources of safety data included the two 12-week studies, as well as a long-term, dedicated safety trial with a planned 52-week treatment period.
As with other PAMORAs, Gastrointestinal (GI) events represented the majority of adverse reactions associated with naldemedine treatment (i.e., events reasonably attributable to treatment). 236 of 1163 (10.3%) naldemedine-treated subjects experienced \ge 1 of the 4 major GI reactions (abdominal pain, diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting), of which >50% reported onset of symptoms within the first 7 days of treatment (n=122, 10.4), and >30% reported onset on day 1 (n=71, 6.1%). The most common ARs were abdominal pain and diarrhea, occurring in 10.4% and 8.9% of naldemedine-treated subjects (vs. 4.5% and 3.4% of placebo), respectively. GI adverse reactions led to early study discontinuation in 38 naldemedine subjects (3.3%). However, SAEs for GI reactions were rare (n=6 naldemedine subjects), and 3/6 of subjects subsequently completed treatment.
Forty-six cases of opioid withdrawal were identified either by investigator report or retrospective chart review—31 in naldemedine patients vs. 15 in placebo patients. No significant difference in pain (either pain scores or AEs) between groups was determined; however, methodology was not adequate for conclusive assessments.
Regarding other labelled class-wide safety concerns, 1 case of hypersensitivity was reported and resulted in hospitalization and treatment discontinuation. No gastrointestinal perforations occurred, although 2 naldemedine subjects were diagnosed with gastric ulcerations (vs. 0
medications approved for treatment of OIC. GI reactions comprised the majority of ARs, and led to study discontinuation in 5% of all naldemedine-treated subjects (30% of subjects experiencing GI ARs.) However, serious events were rare and symptoms resolved without sequelae upon treatment discontinuation, and some subjects had spontaneous resolution despite ongoing treatment. Therefore, these reactions appear to be more of a tolerability issue, rather than a severe safety issue.
The naldemedine safety evaluation did not identify any new or unexpected safety signals or serious reactions, including risk of MACE, attributable to treatment.
The naldemedine safety database represents the largest prospective collected dataset of randomized, double-blind treatment to date among the PAMORAs. Safety assessments for all phase 3 trials included prospective identification and adjudication of MACE. Although the safety data did not demonstrate an increased frequency of MACE compared to placebo, the safety database from controlled phase 3 trials of naldemedine was too small to generate to yield an event frequency high

This application also included results of specific safety assessments to assess the risk of major adverse cardiac events (MACE), including prospective adjudication by an independent committee, in order to address a class-wide safety concern arising from the long-term safety trial alvimopan (Enterreg), a PAMORA not indicated for OIC and approved only for short-term use. An increased frequency of MACE compared to placebo was not observed in phase 3 trials [5 adjudicated MACE cases in the nalmedemine arm (vs. 6 in placebo; RR 0.835, 95% CI 0.256, 2.728), and 8 cases when all sudden cardiopulmonary deaths and 1 un-adjudicated myocardial infarction are included (vs. 7 in placebo; RR 1.145, 95% CI 0.418, 3.137).
The primary molecular and physiologic considerations relevant to PAMORA toxicity are off-target binding of opioid receptors (all subtypes) and the potential for CNS penetration, particularly in patients with disruptions of the BBB. Unlike exogenous opioids, opioid receptor binding by nalmedemine and other opioid antagonists is not restricted to the mu (\mu) subclass of opioid receptors, though relative binding and degree of agonist/antagonist activity of the receptor subtypes [\mu (\mu), delta (\delta), and kappa (\kappa)] vary among PAMORAs.

L44: ## 2 Therapeutic Context
L45: ## Analysis of Condition
L46: Opioid analgesics represent one of the most prescribed classes of medications worldwide and are commonly prescribed for the management of moderate to severe chronic pain. The estimated prevalence of chronic pain is 15% in the adult US population, and approximately one-third are prescribed opioid analgesics. [6] Reports estimate that more than 3% of American adults are undergoing opioid therapy for more than 3 months for chronic noncancer pain.[1] OIC is the most frequent opioid side effect, affecting at least 40% of opioid-treated patients[4] Unlike other opioid side effects, such as sedation and respiratory depression, patients typically do not develop tolerance to the gastrointestinal side effects of opioids with prolonged exposure. Instead, OIC typically persists with ongoing opioid treatment and progressively worsens with increased opioid doses, often despite aggressive treatment with laxatives and stool softeners.[3]
L47: Common symptoms of OIC include abdominal pain, cramping, and bloating pain, lumpy or hard stools, sensation of incomplete evacuation, and sensation of anorectal obstruction.[6] Although life-threatening complications are rare, OIC has many significant and clinically important sequelae that negatively impact patients' quality of life, including anorexia, poor nutrition, fecal impaction, fecal incontinence, pseudo-obstruction, impaired drug absorption, and urine retention. In addition, OIC can be a dose-limiting side effect and lead to inadequate pain management. [4,5]
L48: Opioid receptors, predominantly \mu sub-type, are densely distributed throughout the myenteric plexus and submucosal plexus of the stomach, small intestine, and large intestine and are involved in several neural pathways responsible for GI motility and secretion of fluid and electrolytes. Activation of \mu-opioid receptors disrupts the coordinated, peristaltic contractions of the GI tract, causing delayed gastric emptying and slowed intestinal transit. In addition, \mu-opioid receptor activation decreases stool water content by decreasing secretion of water and electrolyte into the gut lumen and increasing fluid absorption from the colon. Furthermore, opioids increase ileocecal and anal sphincter tone and impair the defecation reflex through reduced sensitivity to distension and increased internal anal sphincter tone. [5]
L49: ## 2.2. Analysis of Current Treatment Options
L50: Currently, there are three approved drugs for the treatment of OIC, two of which are in the class of peripherally-acting opioid receptor antagonists (PAMORAs)—Relistor (methylnaltrexone bromide), available as a subcutaneous injection and oral tablet, and Movantik (naloxegol), an oral tablet for oral administration. The third approved drug for OIC is
L51: Amitiza (lubiprostone), a chloride channel activator that is also approved for the treatment of chronic idiopathic constipation (CIC) and irritable bowel syndrome (IBS). These FDA-approved drugs for treatment of OIC are listed in Table 1.

L72: ## Regulatory History- Pediatric Study Plan
L73: ## *Pediatric Studies in OIC:*
L74: The proposed indication for treatment of OIC in patients with chronic, non-cancer pain has been included in the FDA list of “adult-Related Conditions that qualify for a waiver because they rarely or never occur in pediatrics” because studies would be impossible or highly impractical.
L75: This qualification is based primarily on the Agency's determination that extrapolation of adult clinical trial data would not be sufficient to establish efficacy of OIC treatments in children, and pediatric studies with \ge 4 weeks of concomitant study drug/opioid treatment would be required to adequately assess treatment effect. However, an FDA working group assembled specifically to assess opioid usage in children failed to identify a population of pediatric patients who are likely to (1) be treated with chronic, round-the-clock opioid therapy; (2) develop constipation as a sequela of opioid use that is unresponsive to dietary modifications; (3) and require sustained opioid dosages of for \ge 4 weeks during study participation.
L76: (b) (4)
L77: Consequently, the Agency has reached consensus that pediatric trials for OIC in chronic non-cancer pain are considered highly impracticable due to the low numbers of patients, and “OIC in chronic, non-cancer pain” has been listed among the PeRC-accepted “adult-related conditions that qualify for a waiver because they rarely or never occur in pediatrics.”
L78: For additional details, refer to the DPMH memo by Ethan Hausman submitted on 18 September 2015 under IND 107475.

L86: ## Presubmission Regulatory History-Cardiovascular Risk Assessment
L87: During the EOP2 Meeting on 19 February 2013, the Division communicated that a cardiovascular outcomes trial (CVOT) would be needed to be conducted prior to approval to assess for an increased risk of major adverse cardiac events (MACE) with chronic naldemedine administration. The basis for the Division's concern was the identification of a possible cardiovascular safety signal (MACE) during clinical trials of other members of the drug class of peripheral opioid receptor antagonists. The Division also noted that naldemedine antagonizes opioid receptor subtypes (e.g., \delta-delta) that are known to be present in the cardiovascular system and that are implicated in cardioprotection. Therefore, the Division stated that the naldemedine CVOT should be designed to include an adequate number of events to perform these safety assessments, and sample size determinations should account for the incidence of MACE and exposure required for the safety dataset, rather than just the number of patients.
L88: To address the need for cardiovascular assessments across the class of peripheral opioid receptor antagonists, a meeting of the Anesthetic and Analgesic Drug Products Advisory Committee was held on 11-12 June 2014. Among the FDA presenters was Dr. Clara Kim (Office of Biostatistics/Division of Biometrics) who spoke about statistical considerations in the design of randomized controlled cardiovascular outcomes trials.
L89: Based on the AC discussions, a post-marketing requirement (PMR) was issued to conduct an observational epidemiologic study of Movantik (naloxegol), for which the clinical development program had been completed and the marketing application was under review at the time of the AC meeting. This PMR has a deadline of June 2018 for interim report submission and December 2023 for final clinical study report submission.
L90: For naldemedine, the Applicant proposed to conduct a pre-approval, long-term (52-week), randomized, placebo-controlled safety trial (Study 1326V9235). A Type C Meeting was held on 18 September 2014 at the request of the Applicant to obtain feedback regarding the design of Study 1326V9235, as well as the adequacy of the overall safety database to support the registration of naldemedine for treatment of OIC. Although Study 1326V9235 was initially intended as a CVOT for assessment of MACE, the primary endpoint was changed to the general safety of naldemedine, with a proposed population size of 1200 subjects (600 randomized to each arm), for which they specified an assumption of a 15% drop-out rate. (This represented a reduction from the original plan to enroll 1500 subjects.) In response, the Division noted that, historically, drop-out rates in long-term safety studies for OIC with chronic non-cancer pain were &gt;30%, and although the sample size was not determined using a specific power calculation, the Division recommended that the Applicant account for a higher number of dropouts than their predicted 15% rate to ensure adequate naldemedine exposure for safety assessments.
L91: The Division of Biometrics VII stated that the proposed trial, Study 1326V9235, to assess cardiovascular risks of naldemedine was not designed to test a statistical hypothesis, and therefore was not statistically powered. The sponsor proposed to summarize the primary endpoint by the proportion of MACE and the corresponding 95% confidence interval (CI) by treatment group. Because this study was considered to be descriptive in nature, the safety biostatistics reviewers did not provide specific comments for the Division or feedback for the Applicant regarding the trial design or size of the proposed study population. (see memo by Ling Lan and Clara Kim dated 29 September 2014)
L92: The issue of adequacy of the safety database for NDA submission to support the registration of naldemedine was addressed again during the pre-NDA meeting on 30 September 2015. The Applicant proposed NDA submission with an interim dataset for Study V9235. During discussions with the Applicant, the Division agreed to NDA submission prior to completion of Study V9235 with a “Core Period Data Set” to be included in the NDA submission, and a “Supplemental Period Data Set” to be submitted in the 120-Day Safety Update (see below). The Division stated that the adequacy of the available data to assess the safety of naldemedine would be evaluated as part of the review process and cautioned the Applicant that submission of insufficient safety data in the NDA could result in a review clock extension.
L93: ## Data Sets for Study V9235, as agreed upon during Pre-NDA Meeting (30 September 2015):
L94: - *Core Period Data Set: to consist of data for all randomized subjects, up to the cutoff date of June 24, 2015, at which time subjects should have completed between 24 and up to 52 weeks of treatment exposure, and will serve as the primary dataset for safety analyses in the Study V9235 Clinical Study Report (CSR) for NDA submission.*
- *Supplemental Period Data Set: to consist of remaining data (i.e. study visits not completed at the time of the Core Period Data Set cutoff) for supplemental safety data to be submitted with the Day-120 safety update report.*

L96: ## 4.1. Office of Scientific Investigations (OSI)
L97: Clinical inspections were conducted at 6 sites, 3 sites who participated in Study V9231 and 3 sites for Study V9232. The sites selected for inspection had not had a recent OSI investigation, were among the sites with highest enrollment, and may have been identified as having efficacy results inconsistent with the overall population data or disproportionate large or small number of reported protocol violations. The sites inspected had enrollment between 14 and 26
L98: subjects. For additional details, the reader is referred to the summary review by Susan Susan Leibenhaut, M.D., dated 22 December 2016.
L99: None of the clinical site inspections identified findings likely to have negatively impacted the integrity of the clinical trial data. Four clinical sites were classified as No Action Indication (NAI), and 2 clinical sites were classified as voluntary action indication (VAI). None of the violations cited were considered to have an impact on data integrity. The reviewer concluded that the studies have been conducted adequately, and the data generated by the studies appear acceptable.
L100: ## 4.2. Product Quality
L101: According to the CMC review team, the applicant provided sufficient data to assure the identity, strength, purity, and quality of the drug product. At the time of this review, there were no ongoing issues regarding product quality. In addition, the Office of Process and Facilities (OPF) recommended “Approval” recommendation for the facilities involved in this application as of this review. The reader is referred to the full CMC review by Joseph Leginus (primary drug substance reviewer) and Sarah Ibrahim (primary drug product review) for a detailed evaluation of product quality.
L102: Naldemedine tosylate is a white to light tan powder, soluble in dimethylsulfoxide and methanol, slightly soluble in alcohol and water. Naldemedine tablets for oral use contain 0.26 mg of naldemedine tosylate equivalent to 0.2 mg naldemedine (S-297995), the primary active moiety. Excipients are D-mannitol, croscarmellose sodium, magnesium stearate, hypromellose, talc, and yellow ferric oxide. Naldemedine tablets are unscored.

L105: ## 4.4. Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
L106: The reader is referred to the nonclinical review by Tracy Behrsing, Ph.D., dated 21 November 2016 for a full discussion of naldemedine nonclinical study data submitted in support of this application. The following is a summary of important findings from the nonclinical program, with particular attention to information to be included in product labeling:
L107: - Toxicology: Repeat-dose toxicity studies with naldemedine were conducted in multiple species, with durations of up to 6 months in rats and 9 months in dogs. In the 6-month rat study, systemic exposures (AUC) at the NOAEL (100 mg/kg/day) were approximately 3600 times that in humans at the recommended human dose.
L108: - The liver was the primary target organ of toxicity in repeat dose studies. At doses much higher than the recommended human dose, elevated transaminases and histopathological changes of centrilobular necrosis were observed.
- In the 9-month study in dogs, systemic exposures (AUC) at the NOAEL (4 mg/kg/day) were approximately 345 times that in humans at the recommended human dose. In 1-month toxicity studies in rats, prolongation of diestrous occurred at doses as low as 0.3 mg/kg/day. While a NOEL for this effect (which may be related to treatment-related increases in prolactin) was not identified, recovery of the estrous cycle generally occurred even during the dosing period. No significant gross pathological findings.
- Distribution: In a study of drug distribution with radiolabeled naldemedine, penetration across the blood brain barrier into the CNS was not observed.
- Carcinogenesis: The carcinogenic potential of naldemedine was evaluated in 2 studies—a 2-year in mice and a 2-year study in rat using naldemedine doses up to 100 mg/kg/day, for which exposures were approximately 17,500 and 6,300 times the human exposures (AUC) at the recommended human dose in mice and rats, respectively. No evidence of carcinogenic potential was observed in either of these studies.
- Mutagenesis: Naldemedine was not genotoxic in a bacterial reverse mutation (Ames) assay, a chromosomal aberration assay with cultured Chinese hamster lung cells, and an in vivo rat bone marrow micronucleus assay.
- Fertility/embryofetal development: A species-specific prolongation of diestrous phase was noted in rats at a dose of 10 mg/kg/day in female rats (AUC approximately 180 times the AUC in humans at the recommended human dose). Overall, no effects on fertility or reproductive performance were observed in either male or female rats at oral doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day (approximately 17,000 times the human exposures (AUC) at the recommended human dose). In embryo-fetal development studies, increased abortion/premature deliveries were observed in rabbits treated with 400 mg/kg/day (844 times the human AUC at the recommended human dose). These events were attributed to marked decreases in maternal food consumption and lower fetal body weights. In rats, no adverse effects on embryo or fetal development were observed with doses up to 1000 mg/kg/day. In the pre- and postnatal development study in rats given naldemedine during the postimplantation through weaning at the dose up to 1000 mg/kg/day, one dam died at parturition at 1000 mg/kg/day and poor nursing; decrease in body weight gain and decrease in food consumption were noted at 30 and 1000 mg/kg/day. Decreases in the viability index on Day 4 after birth were noted at 30 and 1000 mg/kg/day, and low body weights and delayed pinna unfolding in pups were noted at 1000 mg/kg/day. The AUC0-24hr at 30 mg/kg/day was 626 times the human AUC0-τ at the recommended human dose. No adverse effect level on pre- and postnatal
L109: development including maternal function such as maintenance of pregnancy, delivery, and nursing was observed at 1 mg/kg/day, which is 12 times the AUC0-\tau at the recommended human dose.
L110: - At the recommended dose, the proposed specifications for actual and potential impurities and residual solvents comply with limits set by the ICH Q3A guidelines. In addition, the quantities of these compounds detected during batch testing were below the specified limits. Actual and potential impurities formed during the manufacturing process were evaluated for mutagenic potential, and 1 of the 6 impurities (b)(4) was shown to test positive for bacterial mutagenicity.
- In vitro pharmacology: The reported binding affinities of naldemedine for human mu, delta, and kappa opioid receptors (expressed as K_i values) were 0.34, 0.43, and 0.94 nM, respectively. Relative to naldemedine, each of the metabolites studied were less potent or had no binding affinities for mu, delta, and kappa receptors, with the exception of naldemedine 6-O-\beta-D-glucuronide, which had a K_i value of 0.51 nM for the delta receptors subtype. The parent compound has antagonist properties/activity at all subtypes. While nornaldemedine (a major metabolite in humans) showed weak agonistic activity for delta receptors, the EC_{50} value was approximately 340 times the human C_{max} for this metabolite at the recommended human dose of 0.2 mg once daily.
- Primary pharmacology (rats): naldemedine prevented morphine- and oxycodone-induced suppression of small intestinal transit (ED_{50} values of 0.02 – 0.23 mg/kg) and antagonized the inhibitory effect of morphine on castor oil-induced diarrhea (ED_{50} value of 0.01 mg/kg).
L111: ## 4.5. Clinical Pharmacology
L112: The dose-response and exposure-response data from naldemedine clinical trials were considered adequate to support the effectiveness of the proposed naldemedine dose and dosing regimen to treat OIC in patients with chronic, non-cancer pain. In addition, adequate data were available to determine appropriate dosing in special populations and evaluate drug-drug interactions. The following is a summary of the Clinical Pharmacology findings to support dosing and labeling recommendations for naldemedine. The reader is referred to the full Clinical Pharmacology review by Sandhya Apparaju for greater detail.
L113: Preliminary evidence of effectiveness, proof-of-concept, and dose-range guidance for phase 3 naldemedine clinical trials in the proposed population was obtained from a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group, phase 2b dose-ranging study (Study V9221) that evaluated 3 dosages of naldemedine (0.1 mg, 0.2 mg and 0.4 mg once daily for 28 days) in a population of subjects with OIC and chronic non-cancer pain. Efficacy (change in the frequency of SBMs per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the treatment period) was
L114: evaluated. Mean changes from baseline in SBMs per week relative to placebo were significantly different for the 0.2 mg and 0.4 mg doses (increase of 3.38 and 3.63 SBMs per week vs. 1.42 in placebo) but not for the 0.1 mg dose (increase of 1.99 SBMs per week). The efficacy at the two higher doses was comparable (not significantly different), but significantly different from the 0.1 mg group. Similar trends were noted for the key secondary endpoints including the mean change in the frequency of SBMs from baseline to Weeks 1, 2, 3 and 4 of the trial, the change from baseline in the frequency of complete spontaneous bowel movements (CSBMs), the proportion of SBM responders during the last 2 weeks of the treatment period and the time to first spontaneous bowel movement after the initial administration of study drug. Data for these secondary endpoints support significant but comparable efficacy at the 0.2 mg and 0.4 mg doses, and a modest but not statistically significant effect at the 0.1 mg dose of naldemedine. Overall, the dose-response for primary and secondary efficacy outcomes in this trial provided supportive evidence of effectiveness of the 0.2 mg dose of naldemedine.
L115: ## 4.5.1. **Mechanism of Action**
L116: Naldemedine is a naltrexone derivative, with a large polar surface area that reduces its ability to access the central nervous system. It acts as a peripherally-acting \mu-opioid receptor antagonist (PAMORA) and blunts the effect of opioid drugs at their peripheral receptors, in particular the enteric nervous system in the GI tract. In this way naldemedine reverses the constipating effects of opioids, thereby improving bowel movements, stool consistency and reducing straining.
L117: ## 4.5.2. **Pharmacodynamics**
L118: In single-ascending dose studies, naldemedine was well tolerated at up to 100 mg. Doses up to 30 mg for 10 days were also well tolerated in multiple ascending dose trials. However, it is important to note that these studies were conducted in healthy individuals without concomitant opioid treatment and sustained opioid exposure prior to treatment with naldemedine. The tolerability profile of opioid receptor antagonism by naldemedine would be expected to differ in individuals with chronic opioid exposure.
L119: In the phase 2a, single dose, proof of concept trial, naldemedine was administered over a 300-fold dose range (0.01 mg to 3 mg). A dose-related increase in SBMs during the 24 hours following a single dose of naldemedine was observed, particularly at 0.3 mg or higher doses (Figure 3). The proportion of subjects who reported a GI-related TEAE increased in a dose-dependent manner, with an increase in frequency observed beginning at a dose of 0.3 mg or higher. Based on these data, doses of 0.1 mg, 0.2 mg, and 0.4 mg were selected for the Phase 2b trial. Although the clinical efficacy after a single dose of 0.1 mg was not robust or statistically significant in this trial, this dose was included because it was well-tolerated and the potential for additional efficacy after multiple daily doses was of interest.
L120: **Figure 2: Dose-Response for Efficacy in the Phase 2a Proof-Of-Concept Trial of Naldemedine**
L121: (Source: Figure 4, *Clinical Pharmacology Review for NDA 208854* by Dr. Sandhya Apparaju, page 14/84)
Abbreviations used in figure: CSBM, mg, SBM
L122: In the phase 2 dose-ranging trial, all tested doses of naldemedine (0.1 mg, 0.2 mg, and 0.4 mg) were associated with an increase in SBM frequency from baseline that was greater than placebo, but results for the 0.1 mg dose did not achieve statistical significance (Figure 4). No additional benefit was noted at the 0.4 mg dose (compared to the 0.2 mg dose), and this higher dose was associated with a higher adverse event (AE) rate (Figure 6). Similar differences in
L123: dose-response were observed for dose-group SBM responder rates, as defined for the phase 3 primary efficacy endpoint (Figure 5). Based on these data, phase 3 trials were designed using only the 0.2 mg dose.
L124: **Figure 3: Dose-Response for Efficacy Outcomes in the Phase 2b Dose-Ranging Trial (Change in SBM Frequency from Baseline)**
L125: (Source: Figure 3, Clinical Pharmacology Review for NDA 208854 by Dr. Sandhya Apparaju, page 13/84)

L127: (Source: Figure 4, Clinical Pharmacology Review for NDA 208854 by Dr. Sandhya Apparaju, page 14/84)

L129: (Source: Figure 6, Clinical Pharmacology Review for NDA 208854 by Dr. Sandhya Apparaju, page 15/84)
L130: The Clinical Pharmacology reviewer concluded that the dose and exposure response relationships for safety (i.e. gastrointestinal disorders, abdominal pain, and diarrhea) suggest that the 0.2 mg dose is acceptable.
L131: ## 4.5.3. Pharmacokinetics
L132: ## Summary of ADME PK Results
L133: Naldemedine PK parameters were comparable in healthy volunteers and OIC patients.
L134: Naldemedine exposures (C_{max} and AUC) increased in a nearly dose proportional manner following single and multiple daily doses. Steady-state was reached within two days and a modest accumulation was noted in healthy, and accumulation in OIC patients was minimal.
L135: PK variability in healthy volunteers was small to moderate across the various parameters (14 % to 25 %), while it was somewhat higher in OIC patients in the phase 2b trial (22 % - 48 %).
L136: Naldemedine was not removed by dialysis. The fraction of naldemedine bound to human serum proteins is 93.2% to 94.2%, primarily to albumin. There is no concentration-dependence of the protein binding in the range from 0.02 to 2 µg/mL. Naldemedine has a terminal elimination T_{1/2} of ~ 9 to 11 hours.
L137: Naldemedine is primarily metabolized by CYP3A4 to form nor-naldemedine, with some contribution by UGT1A3 to form both naldemedine 3-glucuronide (3-G) and naldemedine 6-G. Naldemedine and major metabolite (nor-naldemedine) do not cause clinically relevant induction or inhibition of major CYP450 enzymes.
L138: Naldemedine is also a sensitive substrate of p-glycoprotein (p-gp). No relevant inhibition of major transporters was noted. (See drug-drug interactions below)
L139: Naldemedine is absorbed with a median T_{max} of ~ 0.75 hours post-dose. T_{max} is prolonged in fed state from a median value 0.75 h to 2.5 h. However, these effects were not considered to be of sufficient magnitude to recommend administration instructions based on these findings. Therefore, administration of naldemedine without regard to food intake is acceptable.
L140: Fecal excretion was the major route of elimination (64.3 % of total radioactivity), and mean recovery was ~ 20 % in urine.
L141: ## Drug-Drug Interactions (DDI)
L142: In *in vitro* studies, naldemedine and its major metabolite did not inhibit or induce major CYP450 enzymes. In addition, the drug and metabolite did not inhibit major transporters.
L143: Because naldemedine is a CYP3A4 to nor-naldemedine and it is also a sensitive p-gp substrate, *in vivo* drug-drug interaction studies were conducted to evaluate the effect of moderate and strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (fluconazole and itraconazole), a strong CYP3A4 inducer (rifampin), and a p-gp inhibitor (cyclosporine) on the PK of naldemedine and its major metabolite. PBPK modeling approach was used to evaluate untested DDI scenarios on naldemedine PK.
L144: Table 3 shows the DDI study results, the Applicant's proposed naldemedine dosing based on DDI studies, and the Clinical Pharmacology Review Team's dosing recommendations.
L145: In the presence of the strong CYP3A4 inducer rifampin, a marked decrease in the C_{max} and AUC of naldemedine were observed. Therefore, the Clinical Pharmacology reviewer agreed with the proposed recommendation to avoid dosing of naldemedine with strong CYP3A4 inducers due to the likelihood of decreased efficacy.
L146: The Applicant also proposed [REDACTED] (b) (4) However, only ~1-fold and ~3-fold increases in C_{max} and AUC, respectively, were observed, with co-administration of itraconazole. In addition, adverse event and study discontinuation rates in subjects receiving CYP3A and/or p-gp inhibitors inhibitor did not differ markedly from those not receiving these medications (see Tables 4-7). From an exposure-response perspective, patients with concomitant CYP3A4 inhibitors in the phase 3 Trials 231 and 232 did not appear to exhibit a higher adverse event rate for GI disorders, including abdominal pain, nausea/vomiting, and diarrhea AEs. Together, the Clinical Pharmacology
L147: Review team did not consider naldemedine dosage adjustment or avoidance of treatment in patients receiving CYP3A4 inhibitors (either moderate or severe) or p-gp inhibitors to be warranted.

L167: ## Exposure-Response
L168: Exposure-response data for the PK/PD analysis population from Studies V9231 and V9232 (Naldemedine 0.2 mg: n=445, placebo: n=548) are shown in Table 8. In this table, proportions of SBM responders (primary efficacy endpoint; see Section 7.1.1 for endpoint definition) are presented by AUC group [placebo (0 ng·hr/mL) and 7 AUC ranges—0 to &lt;10, 10 to &lt;15, 15 to &lt;20, 20 to &lt;25, 25 to &lt;30, 30 to &lt;35, ≥35 ng·hr/mL].
L169: With the exception of the lowest AUC group (&lt;10 ng·hr/mL), the proportions of SBM responders were &gt;50% and similar to the SBM responder rates in the naldemedine arm of the primary analysis population (ITT) for Studies V9231 and V9232 (47.6% and 52.5%, respectively).
L170: In the lowest naldemedine AUC group (&lt;10 ng·hr/mL), the proportion of SBM responders was 16.7%, which was also lower than responder rate in the placebo group (34.1%). However, this group only consisted of 24 subjects (5.4% of the naldemedine patients).
L171: For the 10 to &lt;15 ng·hr/mL AUC group, in which patients showed ~ 50% lower systemic exposure compared with the mean AUC of 27.50 ng·hr/mL, the proportion of SBM responders
L172: (61.3%) was also within the 50-60% proportion of SBM responders, as observed in the groups of subjects with AUC &gt;15 ng·hr/mL.

L189: The primary sources of clinical data for this NDA are three phase 3 clinical trials conducted in patients with OIC and chronic non-cancer pain—Studies 1314V9231, 1315V9232, and 1326V9235. Hereafter, these clinical trials will be referred to by their abbreviated names (V9231, V9232, and V9235, respectively), in the manner used by the Applicant in Clinical Study Reports and Clinical Summaries.
L190: Studies V9231, V9232, and V9235 are all multicenter, randomized, double-blind, parallel group trials. Studies V9231 and V9232 are replicate 12-week trials conducted to evaluate the safety and efficacy of naldemedine in adults with OIC and chronic non-cancer pain. Study V9235 is an ongoing, 52-week trial with the primary objective to assess the long-term safety of naldemedine in this patient population.
L191: A tabular listing of the other 19 naldemedine clinical trials is located in Appendix 13.4.
L192: ## 5.2. Review Strategy
L193: The efficacy evaluation of naldemedine for treatment of OIC in patients with chronic non-cancer pain was based primarily on clinical trial data from Studies V9231 and V9232, two replicate phase 3 efficacy studies. Supportive efficacy data were also submitted from the long-term safety trial (Study V9235).
L194: ## Studies V9231 and V9232
L195: For the clinical review, the body of evidence used to determine the effectiveness of naldemedine for the treatment of OIC in adults with chronic, non-cancer pain included data for the protocol-defined primary endpoint and 4 key secondary endpoints.
L196: The primary endpoint for these 2 trials was based on a pre-specified responder definition that was consistent with the primary endpoint used in other clinical development programs for OIC and similar treatment indications and is considered to represent a clinically meaningful treatment effect.
L197: For each of the 4 key secondary endpoints, the analysis consisted of a comparison of group means for the change from baseline in frequency of a pre-defined bowel movement (BM) event at a specified timepoint, which, unlike the primary endpoint, lacks inherent clinical meaningfulness. Based on recommendations provided by Clinical Outcomes Assessment (COA) team, the Division requested that the Applicant conduct correlations of secondary endpoint data with subject global satisfaction (SGS) scores, in an effort to link these data with a measure of how patients feel or function. For each of the 3 endpoints which compared BM event frequency at baseline and the last 2 weeks of the treatment period, cumulative distribution function (CDF) plots of change in BM event frequency were developed, one set with curves for SGS score (pooled treatment arms) and one set with curves for treatment groups. Unfortunately, closer evaluation of the SGS assessment itself revealed that the wording of the SGS query was not adequate to ensure that patient's satisfaction rating was specific to their perceived change in constipation-related symptoms. The COA team concluded that interpretability of these correlations as an indication of the clinical meaningfulness of secondary outcome assessments may have been compromised.
L198: For this application, consideration was also given to prior results from clinical trials of other PAMORAs in order to provide a context in which to assess the clinical meaningfulness of the responder rates and treatment effect size observed in these trials.
L199: ## Study V9235
L200: Unlike Studies V9231 and V9232, Study V9235 did not have a pre-specified primary efficacy endpoint. The Applicant collected efficacy data throughout the trial. However, because specified time points for efficacy endpoints were not pre-specified and variability in
L201: concomitant laxative use was permitted without adequate methods to control for treatment differences, efficacy data from this trial were not pivotal to the overall determination of effectiveness.
L202: ## 6 Review of Relevant Individual Trials Used to Support Efficacy
L203: ## Study V9231
L204: ## 6.1.1. Study Design
L205: ## Overview and Objective
L206: **Title:** A Randomized, Double-blind, Placebo-controlled, Parallel-group Study of Naldemedine in the Treatment of Opioid-induced Constipation (OIC) in Subjects with Non-malignant Chronic Pain Receiving Opioid Therapy
L207: **Study Objectives:** To evaluate the efficacy and safety of naldemedine compared to placebo for the treatment of OIC in adults receiving chronic opioid treatment for chronic, non-cancer pain.
L208: Study V9231 was conducted from 29 August 2013 (date of enrollment of first patient) through 22 January 2015 (completion of the last subject).
L209: ## Trial Design
L210: Study V9321 was a phase 3, randomized, double-blind, parallel-group clinical trial comparing treatment with naldemedine 0.2 mg once daily with placebo. (Note that Study V9232 had the same clinical trial design as Study V9231.)
L211: The total length of trial participation was 18-20 weeks, divided into 3 periods:
L212: - Screening Period: 2- to 4-weeks, including a 14-consecutive-day qualifying period
- Treatment Period: 12-week double-blind, placebo-controlled period of study treatment
- Follow-up Period: 4-week observation period without study treatment
L213: This reviewer considers the randomized, double-blind, parallel-group trial design of Study V9231 (and V9232) to represent an adequate and well-controlled clinical trial design to serve as the basis of the efficacy evaluation for naldemedine for the proposed indication.
L214: ## Study Population
L215: The planned study population was approximately 540 subjects, randomized in a 1:1 ratio to one of 2 treatment arms (270 subjects each) — naldemedine 0.2 mg or placebo once daily.
L216: ## Study Eligibility Assessments and Criteria to Define OIC and Minimum Opioid Usage
L217: To determine study eligibility, an evaluation of subjects' baseline constipation status (i.e., BM frequency and consistency, need for rescue medication, presence of specified constipation-related symptoms) and intake of opioid medications [i.e., average today daily dosage (TDD)] was performed over a 14-consecutive-day qualifying period as part of the study Screening Period and prior to study treatment randomization. These baseline evaluations were based on subjects' daily self-reports of OIC-related data, submitted using an eDiary format, using the same procedures as efficacy data collection during the subsequent Treatment Period.
L218: Prior to initiation of the trial, subjects were required to discontinue all laxative use (e.g., stool softener, bulking agents, GI tract stimulants, prokinetic agents, suppositories, enemas, or other medications/agents used to treat constipation). Subjects were provided with bisacodyl tablets, which were the only laxatives permitted during study participation for use as rescue treatment. Rescue treatment was permitted after a period of 72 hours without BM, as instructed in the Rescue Laxative Guidelines of the Study Protocol.
L219: To be eligible for participation in Study V9231, subjects were required to meet pre-specified criteria for OIC diagnosis and opioid usage. This reviewer considers the pre-specified criteria appropriate and adequately defined to ensure enrollment of (1) subjects with an accurate diagnosis of OIC, (2) a study population that adequately reflects the intended treatment population, and (3) subjects with baseline disease severity sufficient for evaluation of treatment effect.
L220: ## OIC-Related Eligibility Criteria
L221: (A subject had to meet all criteria to be eligible for participation in Study V0231)
L222: ## OIC-Related Eligibility Criteria
L223: *(A subject had to meet all criteria to be eligible for participation in Study V0231)*
L224: - Diagnosis of OIC (by self-report), defined as:Frequency of SBM of:\le 4 spontaneous bowel movements (SBM), total over the 14 days
AND
- \le 3 SBMs in a given week of the qualifying period
AND
- Presence of \ge 1 of the following bowel symptoms in \ge 25\% of BMs:
L225: - Presence of straining
- Lumpy or hard stools
- Sensation of incomplete evacuation
- Sensation of anorectal obstruction/blockage
- Documented medical history of chronic non-cancer pain for which the subject has received treatment with opioid medication for \ge 3 months duration
- Stable opioid regimen for \ge 1 month prior to screening
- Average total daily dose (TDD) of opioid medication \ge 30 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate
L226: Because the primary data source of efficacy data for Study V9231 was the subject eDiary, subject compliance with eDiary procedures was crucial to ensure collection of adequate data for efficacy analyses. Therefore, evaluation of study eligibility during the 14-consecutive-day qualifying period also included an assessment of subjects' ability to understand and comply with eDiary procedures, which included a minimum required compliance rate of 78% (i.e., completion of \ge 11 of 14 daily eDiary entries during the qualifying period).
L227: ## Study Definitions
L228: ## Bowel Movement Events
L229: - Spontaneous Bowel Movement (SBM): a BM that occurs without the use of a rescue laxative medication during the 24 hours prior to the BM.
*Note that any number of SBMs with a Bristol Stool Scale rating of "1" within a 2-hour period was counted as a single SBM.*
- Complete Spontaneous Bowel Movement (CSBM): a BM without rescue laxative use in the prior 24 hours that is accompanied by feeling of complete evacuation.
*Assessments of CSBM frequency were performed in the same manner as SBM assessments.*
- Frequency of SBMs per week: A calculation of SBM frequency based on the number of SBMs reported, corrected for the number of eDiary entries for the week, using the following formula:
L230: \text{Frequency of SBMs per week} = \frac{\text{Total number of SBMs in the week} \times 7}{\text{\# days of observation related to defecation in the week}}
L231: [The "number of days of observation related to defecation" was defined as the number of eDiary entries containing the requisite efficacy data related to defecation for the week. A minimum of 4 eDiary entries related to defecation was required for a treatment week to be evaluable.]
L232: The definitions for SBM and CSBM used for efficacy assessments in Study V9231 are consistent with terminology used across clinical trials for OIC and other constipation disorders. However, the definition of "frequency of SBMs per week" used for baseline and efficacy assessments was
L233: a derived value which adjusted the observed number of SBM in a week based on the number of completed eDiary entries. The rationale for derivation of adjusted values for efficacy assessments, rather than using the actual number of observed (i.e., subject-reported) SBMs, was to account for the relatively high rate of missing and/or incomplete eDiary data, which the Applicant attributed to challenges related to daily eDiary requirements.
L234: However, the appropriateness of adjustment of SBM frequencies relies on an assumption that SBMs were equally likely occur on days for which a subject did not complete an eDiary entry as days with a completed entry. However, this reviewer is concerned that completion of eDiary entries may be subject to response bias, whereby subjects are more likely to complete eDiary entries in the case of events, such as SBMs or use of rescue medication, and less likely to complete entries on days without such events. Consequently, adjustment of SBM frequency based on the number of eDiary entries could have resulted in the overestimation of naldemedine treatment effect and magnified the difference in responder proportions between the naldemedine and placebo treatment arms. However, the degree to which efficacy results could be skewed by using the derived values for SBM frequency per week was limited by the requirement of \ge 4 eDiary entries for a week.
L235: The various components of the responder definition used for efficacy analyses and the impact of missing and non-evaluable data on efficacy results are discussed further with respect to pre-specified study endpoints and statistical analyses.
L236: ## Eligibility Criteria
L237: The following is a summary of all key study eligibility criteria:
L238: ► Key Inclusion Criteria:

- Age 18 to 80 years of age, inclusive, at the time of informed consent
- Meets criteria for a diagnosis of OIC* and has documented chronic non-cancer pain for which the subject has been receiving opioid treatment for \ge 3 months (see above*)
- Stable opioid regimen at a TDD on average of \ge 30 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate for \ge 1 month prior to Screening (see above*)
- Not taking laxatives at the time of screening or is willing to discontinue laxative use (e.g., stool softener, bulking agents, GI tract stimulants, prokinetic agents, suppositories, enemas, or other medications/agents used to treat constipation); and willing to use only the rescue laxatives provided during the Screening and Treatment Periods
- Met assessment criteria from the 14-consecutive-day qualifying period, related to (1) BM frequency, (2) presence of related bowel symptoms, and (3) eDiary compliance, as defined above
- Meets the following reproductive-related criteria:
L239: - For males: either sterile for 6 months prior to Screening (e.g., vasectomy) or agreed to use an approved method of contraception (use of a condom with spermicide from the start of the Screening Visit until 28 days after the last dose of study drug)
- For females: non-pregnant, non-lactating, and either postmenopausal (defined as cessation of regular menstrual periods for \ge 1 year if \ge 50 years old or 2 years if &lt; 50 years old with FSH confirmation), surgically sterile by hysterectomy, bilateral oophorectomy, or agreed to use a highly effective (with failure rates &lt; 1\% per year) technique of birth control from the Screening Visit until 28 days after the last dose of study drug; females of child-bearing potential must have had a negative serum pregnancy test at the Screening Visit and a negative urine pregnancy test at Visit 2 (Day 1) prior to dosing
- For both sexes: subjects who were not sexually active (abstinent) must have agreed to use one of the above-mentioned methods if they became sexually active while participating in the study
L240: ## ► Key Exclusion Criteria:
L241: - Significant structural abnormalities of the GI tract:History of bowel obstructions, stricture
- History of bowel surgery such as bowel resection or bariatric surgeries
- Active medical diseases affecting bowel transit such as ileus, uncontrolled hypothyroidism, irritable bowel syndrome, inflammatory bowel disease (ulcerative colitis, Crohn's disease, diverticulitis), fecal incontinence
- History or presence of pelvic disorders that may have been a cause of constipation
- Surgery (except for minor procedures) within 60 days of Screening, or planned surgery during study treatment that would have influenced pain or bowel function or precluded completion of the study
- History of chronic constipation prior to starting analgesic medication or any potential non-opioid cause of bowel dysfunction that may have been a major contributor to the constipation (e.g., mechanical GI obstruction)
- Severe constipation that had not been appropriately managed such that the subject was at immediate risk of developing serious complications of constipation. This included subjects who had reported no BMs for 7 consecutive days prior to and during the Screening Period
- Subjects who had never taken laxatives for the treatment of OIC
- Initiation of a new bowel regimen or prokinetic agent, or significant change in diet supplement use, or exercise regimens within 28 days of the Screening Visit
L242: - History of active treatment for cancer within the last 2 years of Screening (except for basal cell or squamous cell carcinoma of the skin that had been successfully resected, or tamoxifen and raloxifene when being used for prevention of breast cancer)
- Abnormal screening laboratory test result(s), including:Alanine aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate aminotransferase (AST) &gt;2 times the upper limit of normal (ULN)
- Total bilirubin &gt;1.5 times the ULN
- Serum creatinine &gt;1.5 times the ULN
- Significant comorbid medical condition(s), including but not limited to cardiovascular (CV), respiratory, hepatic, renal, or endocrine dysfunction, or human immunodeficiency infection, to the extent that study participation is not considered to be subject's best interest or may interfere with study compliance/completion.
- Significant CV risk such as presence or suspected presence of unstable coronary artery disease, myocardial infarction, stroke, or transient ischemic attack within 3 months of Screening
- Any medical or psychiatric condition that, in the opinion of the Investigator, may have compromised the ability of the subject to understand and comply with the study protocol or provide informed consent to participate in the study
- Positive results for serologic tests for hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg), hepatitis C virus (HCV), or active hepatitis A virus (HAV)
- Use at Screening of any prohibited medication, including opioid antagonists, partial agonists, or mixed agonist/antagonists
- History of illegal drug use in the last 2 years of Screening, documented misuse of prescribed medications or a positive urine drug screen, with the exception of findings consistent with administration of prescribed medications
- Allergic reaction to opioids or known or suspected hypersensitivity to naltrexone hydrochloride, methylnaltrexone bromide, naloxone hydrochloride, alvimopan, or any other opioid antagonist
- Treatment with an investigational product within 2 months prior to the Screening Visit
- Prior participation in naldemedine trials
L243: Study eligibility for Study V9231 was generally consistent with the eligibility for participation in clinical trials for similar products, and the pre-specified inclusion/exclusion criteria sufficient reflect the target treatment population for the proposed indication. Similarly, the pre-specified OIC-related definitions and methods for quantifying opioid use were appropriate to ensure enrollment of patients with OIC support the proposed indication.
L244: This reviewer notes, however, that protocol amendments were issued to clarify inclusion criteria and disease-related definitions. Although these protocol changes were instituted after
L245: enrollment had already been initiated, no notable impact on the clinical trial population or efficacy results was noted during this review.
L246: ## Study Treatment
L247: The study drug (naldemedine 0.2 mg or placebo) used for the Treatment Period was supplied in bottles of 36 tablets in double-blinded manner, labeled with the subject's randomization number, number of tablets, cautionary statement, and storage conditions. Clinical trial sites dispensed the medication directly to study subjects during Study Visits.
L248: The naldemedine formulation consisted of an oral tablet, containing 0.2 mg of the active compound. Placebo tablets were identical to active tablets in appearance, taste, color, and odor. Subjects were instructed to take one tablet of the dispensed study drug by mouth once daily, regardless of randomization assignment. Naldemedine was administered without regard to food. Administration instructions also specified that subjects should identify the most appropriate time for dosing each day that would maximize compliance with dosing and convenience relative to the occurrence of BMs, and every effort should be made to take the study drug at approximately the same time each day throughout the Treatment Period. Subjects were required to capture the time of administration of the study drug by eDiary for only the first 14 days of dosing.
L249: ## Dose Selection:
L250: A single naldemedine dosage regimen (0.2 mg once daily, administered as an oral fixed-dose tablet) has been used in all phase 3 clinical trials. No dose modifications have been made during the planning or conduct of naldemedine phase 3 clinical trials.
L251: The rationale for the dose selection of naldemedine for the phase 3 clinical trials to support this NDA was mainly based on the phase 2 clinical trial which evaluated 2 naldemedine doses (0.1 mg and 0.2 mg) vs. placebo.
L252: ## Assignment to Study Treatment:
L253: - Randomization Procedures: Subjects were to be randomized within a maximum of 7 days of meeting the eligibility requirements. At randomization, subjects were stratified based on their documented opioid use (average TDD during the 14-consecutive-day qualifying period of either 30 to 100 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate or &gt;100 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate. Randomization utilized a centralized interactive voice recognition system (IVRS)/Interactive Web Response System (IWRS).
- Blinding Procedures: Subjects, the Investigator, all study personnel, and data analysts were blinded to the treatment assignment until database lock. Treatment assignments for study drug administration were concealed by the use of identical packaging, labeling, dosing instructions, appearance, taste, color, and odor. The randomization schedule was kept
L254: confidential and was only accessible to the Drug Supply Management staff, IVRS/IWRS Clinical Coordinator(s), IVRS/IWRS vendor staff, unblinded statistician on the Data Safety Monitoring Board, and in cases of Suspected Unexpected Serious Adverse Reactions, as required by local regulation, until database unlock.
L255: ## Treatment Compliance:
L256: Assessments of treatment compliance by the Investigator and/or site personnel were performed during each study visit, including:
L257: - Tablet counts performed by qualified site personnel to verify that the correct number of doses were taken based on the actual number of days that had elapsed between clinic visits
- Information provided to the Investigator/staff by the subject during Study Visits
- eDiary records (only collected for the first 14 days of the Treatment Period)
L258: If noncompliance with study medication was identified, site personnel were supposed to counsel subjects, and if non-compliance persisted (dosing compliance &lt;80%), the subject was to be withdrawn from the study. In the event that a subject was withdrawn for compliance reasons, the Investigator was responsible for notification of the Application or designee and to make every effort to complete the Early Termination assessments and the Early Termination/Study Completion eCRF. All subject discontinuations were supposed to be registered in the IVRS/IWRS system.
L259: ## **Prior and Concomitant Medications/Therapies**
L260: All medications (prescription and over-the-counter) taken by the subject for 3 months prior to Screening, and concomitant medications taken with the study drug through the end of the study were recorded in the CRF.
L261: ## Concomitant Opioid Therapy
L262: By definition of the study population, all subjects enrolled in this study were receiving opioid therapy for chronic, non-cancer pain. To be eligible for study participation, a subject was required to have been treated with opioids for ≥3 months, with a stable opioid regimen at a TDD on average of ≥30 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate for ≥1 month prior to Screening and without anticipated changes in their overall opioid regimen during study participation. Opioid therapy was not provided as part of the study, as subjects were required to be on a stable opioid regimen prior to the Screening Visit.
L263: Subjects were permitted to take additional medication (opioid or non-opioid) for breakthrough pain as prescribed by their physician. At the screening visit, all opioid medication(s) taken by the subject during the prior 3 months were recorded on the eCRF. All pain medication(s) (opioid or non-opioid) were recorded by the subject throughout the study.
L264: Use of tramadol and tapentadol were permitted in this study. Tramadol and tapentadol have a dual mechanism of action, including opioid-mediated analgesia. However, these medications are considered weak opioids and are associated with less constipation than other opioids. Therefore, to ensure that subjects were receiving an adequate opioid dose, tramadol and tapentadol were not included in the calculations for either the stable opioid regimen at a TDD on average of \ge 30 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate for eligibility assessment, or stratification by average TDD.
L265: ## Laxative Therapy
L266: Prior to initiation of study participation, subjects must have agreed to discontinue all laxative use (e.g., stool softener, bulking agents, GI tract stimulants, prokinetic agents, suppositories, enemas, or other medications/agents used to treat constipation) from the start of Screening through the end of the Treatment Period. Every attempt was made to limit laxative use during the 24-hour period immediately prior to randomization.
L267: During the Screening and Treatment Periods, subjects were provided bisacodyl tablets by the Applicant for use as rescue laxative therapy. Rescue treatment was permitted if subjects did not have a BM over a period of 72 hours, as outlined in the Rescue Laxative Guideline in the Study Protocol. Use of other laxatives/medications for treatment of constipation was prohibited and could have resulted in premature discontinuation from study participation.
L268: All rescue laxative therapy during the Screening and Treatment Periods was to be documented in the eDiary, including the laxative name, dose taken, and the date and time of administration. Study drug treatment was continued during rescue laxative treatment.
L269: ## Prohibited Concomitant Medications
L270: Use of the following medications was prohibited at any time during study participation and could result in study discontinuation:
L271: - Opioid antagonists (alvimopan, methylnaltrexone bromide)
- Acetylcholine agonists (bethanechol)
- Guanylate cyclase-C agonist (linaclotide)
- 5-HT-4 agonists (prucalopride)
- Prostaglandin (misoprostol)
- Mu receptor partial agonist opioids (meptazinol)
- Mixed agonist/antagonist (nalorphine-like agonist/antagonist opioids; e.g., pentazocine, buprenorphine) Antispasmodics (dicyclomine)
- Antidiarrheals (loperamide)
- Prokinetics (metoclopramide)
- Chloride channel activator (lubiprostone)
L272: Any additional medications that could have had a significant impact on the GI system or bowel habits may have been prohibited at the Investigator's discretion.
L273: ## Other (Non-medication) Study Restrictions/Dietary Restrictions
L274: - Subjects were not to initiate any new diets, supplement changes, or exercise regimes during Screening and throughout the study.
- Manual removal of stool (i.e., manual disimpaction) was not allowed and disqualified a subject from further participation in the study.
L275: ## **Study Endpoints**
L276: ## Primary Endpoint
L277: The primary efficacy endpoint was the proportion of “responders”, in which a “responder” was defined as a subject who achieved both:
L278: - ≥9 “positive-response weeks” out of the 12-week Treatment Period and
- ≥3 “positive-response weeks” out of the last 4 weeks of the 12-week Treatment Period
L279: In which a “positive-response week” was defined as:
L280: - ≥ 3 SBMs per week and
- An increase from baseline of ≥1 SBM per week for that week
L281: As previously mentioned, a minimum of 4 days of eDiary entries related to defecation was required for a treatment week to be considered evaluable. If &lt;4 days of eDiary entries related to defecation were available for a treatment week, that week was considered non-evaluable and was treated as a “non-response” week.
L282: Also, the frequency of SBMs per week was derived using the following formula which adjusts the number of reported SBMs per week for the number of eDiary entries completed:
L283: \text{Frequency of SBMs per week} = \frac{\text{Total number of SBMs in the week} \times 7}{\text{\# days of observation related to defecation in the week}}
L284: These data handling procedures apply to all study endpoints using the variable “Frequency of SBM [or CSBM] per week.”
L285: ## Secondary Endpoints
L286: Four secondary endpoints were pre-specified in the clinical trial protocol:
L287: - Change in the frequency of SBMs per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the Treatment Period (to assess for persistence of effect)
- Change in the frequency of SBMs per week from baseline to Week 1 of the Treatment Period
- Change in the frequency of CSBMs per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the Treatment Period
- Change in the frequency of SBMs without straining per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the Treatment Period
L288: ## **Exploratory Endpoints:**
L289: The Applicant also performed data analyses for the following exploratory endpoints:
L290: - Proportion of complete SBM (CSBM) responders (using the same responder definition as SBM for the primary endpoint
- Proportion of SBM responders in any 6 weeks of the 12-week Treatment Period
- Proportion of SBM Responders in any 9 weeks of the 12-week Treatment Period
- Change in each of the following 8 variables related to defecation per week from baseline to each week of the Treatment Period:Frequency of SBMs with BSS of 3 or 4 per week
- Frequency of SBMs per week
- Frequency of CSBMs per week
- Frequency of SBMs without straining per week
- Frequency of SBMs without blockage per week
- Number of days with at least 1 SBM per week
- Number of days with at least 1 CSBM per week
- Proportion of Responders by Opioid Dose Strata
- Time to the first SBM and CSBM after initial dose of study drug
- Incidences of SBM and CSBM within 4, 8, 12, 24, and 48 hours of initial study drug dose
- Change in maximal number of days between SBMs from baseline for each 2-week period of the Treatment Period
- Change in each variable (see exploratory endpoint 4) related to rescue laxative per week from baseline to each week of the Treatment Period
- Change in the abdominal bloating and abdominal discomfort scores from baseline to each week of the Treatment Period
- Change from baseline in overall and each domain for PAC-SYM and PAC-QOL Change from baseline in overall SF-36 and each domain of SF-36
- Subject Global Satisfaction

L303: ## Subject eDiary
L304: A subject eDiary format (patient self-report, not accessible to the Investigator and other study site personnel) was used during both the Screening and Treatment Periods to obtain constipation-related data for baseline assessments and efficacy analyses of primary and secondary endpoints. The primary assessment tool used to elicit efficacy data from patients via an eDiary was the Bowel Movement and Constipation Assessment (BMCA, shown below), which captured information regarding the frequency, timing, and characteristics of BMs (e.g., consistency, size), as well as the presence of associated symptoms (e.g., straining with BMs, abdominal bloating, abdominal discomfort). The eDiary was also used to record opioid medication use, as well as the time of study drug administration for the first 14 days of the Treatment period.
L305: Blinding of Investigators and other site study personnel to subjects' self-reported efficacy data using platforms such as an eDiary is a beneficial strategy to minimize bias. Nevertheless, the Applicant identified challenges encountered with eDiary use. The Applicant blamed the eDiary format for a higher-than-expected quantity of missing efficacy data, which occurred despite formal assessments of eDiary compliance and proficiency during the Screening period and exclusion of subjects with inadequate eDiary completion rates.
L306: The purported high rate of incomplete eDiary entries was cited as the basis for post-hoc changes in the definitions of the mITT and PP analysis population. Although these late protocol changes carry risk of introduction of bias into study data, their impact was limited by the pre-specified use of the ITT population (a population unaffected by use of these modified definitions) as the primary efficacy analysis population, the implementation of changes prior to database unblinding, and the fact that mITT and PP efficacy analyses were only 2 of 6 planned sensitivity analyses of the primary endpoint.
L307: Assessments conducted using the eDiary:
L308: - Daily assessment of bowel movement events and constipation-related symptoms [using the Bowel Movement and Constipation Assessment (BMCA)- see details below under efficacy assessments]
- Laxative Use
- Opioid Use
L309: ## For Primary and Secondary Endpoint Assessments
L310: ## ➤ **Bowel Movement and Constipation Assessment (BMCA):**
L311: The BMCA was the primary tool used to collect constipation-related data for baseline and efficacy assessments. The BMCA comprised the portion of the eDiary used for daily subject self-reports (eDiary entries) to capture data related to BMs and specific constipation-related symptoms. The following is a list of the components of the BMCA:
L312: - Date and time of BM
- BM Consistency (Score) using the 7-point Bristol Stool Scale (BSS):separate hard lumps like nuts
- sausage shaped but lumpy
- like a sausage but with cracks on its surface
- like a sausage or a snake, smooth and soft
- soft blobs with clear-cut edges
- fluffy pieces with ragged edges/mushy stool
- watery, no solid pieces, entirely liquid
- Feeling of complete emptying after the BM: (yes/no)
- Sensation that stool could not be passed (i.e., presence of a blockage) during BM: (yes/no)
- Severity of straining (score) with the BM:= no straining
- = mild straining
- = moderate straining
- = severe straining
- = very severe straining
- Abdominal bloating severity (score) during the 24-hour period prior to the BM:= absent
- = mild
- = moderate
- = severe
- = very severe
- Abdominal discomfort score for the past 24 hours:= absent
- = mild
- = moderate
- = severe
- = very severe
L313: ## Additional Assessments for Exploratory Endpoints
L314: - Subject Global Satisfaction (SGS)
- Short Form 36 (SF-36): 36-item scale to survey health status and quality of life (QOL) by assessing impact of physical and/or emotional problems on physical activities, social activities, usual role activities, bodily pain, general mental health (psychological distress and well-being), vitality (energy and fatigue), and general health perception
- Patient Assessment of Constipation Symptom (PAC-SYM) and Quality of Life (PAC-QOL) Questionnaire [5-point Likert scale: 0=absence of symptom, 1=mild, 2=moderate, 3=severe, and 4=very severe]
