NDA 208854
Symproic (Naldemedine Tosylate)
L316: - PAC-SYM: 3 domains (abdominal, rectal, and stool symptoms)
- PAC-QOL: 4 domains (physical discomfort, psychological discomfort, worries and concerns, and dissatisfaction)
L317: The schedule of safety assessments is included in Table 10, and assessments are discussed in Section 8.
L318: Note, that of the additional assessments for exploratory endpoints, only SGS data are discussed.
L319: ## Study drug discontinuation
L320: Study drug treatment could be discontinued for any of the following reasons:
L321: - Withdrawal from study participation by subject
- Serious or intolerable adverse event(s) considered to be related to study drug or that the Investigator determined that the subject should have been withdrawn because of safety reasons
- Abnormal liver chemistry tests which meet pre-specified discontinuation criteria
- Lost to follow-up
- Pregnancy
- Protocol deviation that resulted in a significant risk to the subject's safety
- Unblinding
L322: In the event of a subject's withdrawal, the Investigator was responsible for notifying the Applicant through the Interactive Voice/Web Response System (IVRS/IWRS) and to make every effort to complete the end-of-study (or Early Termination) assessments. All subjects who withdrew or discontinued were to be followed until resolution of any adverse events or until the unresolved adverse events were judged by the Investigator to have stabilized.
L323: For subjects who were lost to follow-up (i.e., those subjects whose status was unclear because they failed to appear for study visits without stating an intention to withdraw), the Investigator was to record in the source documents all steps taken to contact the subject, (eg, dates of telephone calls, registered letters, etc.). An Early Termination/Study Completion eCRF was to be completed for all subjects who discontinued prematurely or were lost to follow-up.
L324: ## **Statistical Analysis Plan**
L325: ## Efficacy Analysis Populations:
L326: - Intent-to-Treat (ITT) Population: all randomized subjects.
- Modified ITT (mITT) Population: all randomized subjects who received at least 1 dose of study drug and completed the first 4 weeks of the study with at least 4 days of eDiary entries related to defecation per week
L327: - Per-Protocol Population: all randomized subjects who completed at least 81 days of the 12-week treatment period (i.e., 11 weeks and 4 days, which would be sufficient to count as 12 weeks of data) and did not have any major protocol deviations. (Major protocol deviations were defined as those that could have potentially affected the efficacy conclusions of the study and were determined prior to unblinding.) The ITT population was the prespecified population for efficacy analyses. The ITT and mITT populations were analyzed as randomized.
L328: ## Sample Size Determination
L329: The target study population size of 540 subjects (270 subjects in the active treatment group and 270 subjects in the placebo group) was determined using an assumption of a 45% responder proportion from the naldemedine 0.2 mg group and 30% responder proportion from the placebo group for the ITT Population, which provided &gt;95% power to detect a \ge 15\% difference between groups in responder proportions with a 2-sided significance level of 0.05 by Pearson's chi-squared test.
L330: ## Planned Statistical Analyses
L331: For primary efficacy endpoint analyses, the difference in the responder proportion between the naldemedine and placebo treatment arms was analyzed using the Cochran Mantel Haenszel test, adjusted by the opioid dose strata. The 95% confidence interval (CI) was calculated for the difference between the groups' responder proportion. In addition, the 95% CIs for the responder proportions of each treatment group were calculated using the Clopper-Pearson method.
L332: Subgroup analyses for the primary efficacy endpoint were performed for the following subgroups:
L333: - opioid dose strata
- age
- body mass index (BMI)
- sex
- race
- region (country and site)
L334: Results for subgroup analyses were presented descriptively.
L335: As stated above, efficacy analyses were conducted using the ITT population as the primary analysis population, and missing data were generally handled conservatively, whereby subjects with insufficient data were defined as non-responders, as agreed upon during the End-of-Phase 2 meeting between the Applicant and the Division. In addition, six sensitivity analyses were performed for the primary endpoint to evaluate the impact of missing data, as recommended by the Agency:
L336: - Observed case: Subjects were excluded from the analysis at any week if they had &lt;4 days of eDiary entries related to defecation in that week. A subject who did not have \ge 9 evaluable weeks was considered as a “non-responder.” The frequency of SBMs per week was defined in the same manner as the primary analysis population.
- Complete case: Subjects were excluded from the analysis if they had &lt;4 days of eDiary entries related to defecation at any week. The frequency of SBMs per week was defined in the same manner as the primary analysis population.
- Worst case: If subjects had missing eDiary entries related to defecation at any day in a week, that week was considered as a “non-response” week.
- Modified worst case: If subjects had no entries related to defecation at a day, the number of SBMs at the day was considered as worst value (i.e., 0 SBMs). Specifically, the frequency of SBMs per week was defined as total number of SBMs using only available data for that week. However, if a treatment week was non-evaluable, that week was treated as a “non-response” week.
- Modified intent-to-treat (mITT) Population: The mITT Population was analyzed using the same methods as the primary analysis population.
- Per-Protocol (PP) Population: The Per-Protocol Population was analyzed using the same methods as the primary analysis population.
L337: The reader is referred to the statistical review by George Kordzakhia, Ph.D, additional details.

L352: ## 6.1.2. Study Results
L353: ## Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L354: The Applicant reported that operating procedures and audit programs were by the CRO and Applicant in accordance with the Good Clinical Practice (GCP) guidelines, as documented by the International Conference on Harmonization (ICH) and the FDA.
L355: ## Financial Disclosure
L356: None of the site investigators who participated in this clinical trial had disclosable financial information. Additional details are provided in Section 13.2.
L357: ## Subject Disposition
L358: The subject disposition for all 974 screened subjects in V9231 is shown in Figure 7. Of the 274 subjects randomized to naldemedine, 2 subjects were not dosed (vs. 0 subjects randomized to placebo):
L359: - Subject 12317-010 was randomized but withdrew consent prior to dosing, because the subject did not want to stay at the site for the time required to complete all study procedures for the day of randomization.
- Subject 12322-004 was randomized but discontinued from the study prior to dosing since the site staff was unable to obtain a blood sample.
L360: Study completion rates were similar between the two V9231 treatment arms—85.0% for the naldemedine group and 87.2% for the placebo arm. The number of study discontinuations was slightly higher in the naldemedine group (n=41, 15.0%) vs. the placebo group (n=35, 12.8%).
L361: Notably, the naldemedine group had a higher proportion of subjects who discontinued due to a TEAE (n= 14, 5.1%), accounting for 34% of premature study terminations in the naldemedine arm, compared to the placebo group (n=5, 1.8%), for whom TEAE was cited as the reason for 14% of premature study terminations.
L362: On the other hand, subject withdrawal led to premature study termination in 24 subjects (8.8%) in the placebo arm vs. 16 subjects (5.8%) in the naldemedine arm.
L363: For both arms, discontinuation due to “withdrawal by subject” was the most common reason for premature study termination. Of these, lack of efficacy was cited as the reason for discontinuation for 2 subjects in the naldemedine group vs. 9 subjects in the placebo group. For 6 subjects (4 in the naldemedine group and 2 in the placebo group), no reason for discontinuation was specified. Numbers of subjects lost to follow-up were not notably different between groups (7 in the naldemedine group vs. 5 in the placebo group).
L364: Study discontinuations due to TEAEs are discussed as part of the safety evaluation in Section 8. In addition, a summary of subject disposition and early study termination for all 3 controlled phase 3 studies is shown in Section 7.1.1.

L371: The analysis populations used in Study V9231 are shown in Figure 8, and the reasons for subject exclusion by the Investigator are summarized in Table 11. Overall, more subjects from the naldemedine group were excluded from any analysis population (n=96, 35.0%) vs. the placebo group (n=86, 31.5%). The largest difference between groups was the number of subjects excluded from the mITT population because &lt;4 weeks of the study were completed with \ge 4 days of diary entries per week [32 subjects excluded from the mITT population of the naldemedine arm (11.7%) vs. 18 from the placebo arm (6.6%)]. The higher number of early discontinuations in the naldemedine group due to TEAEs can account for this disparity. This is an important consideration when interpreting primary efficacy data based on the ITT population which classified subjects without minimum required data as non-responders.

L380: ## Protocol Violations/Deviations
L381: A total of 265 major protocol violations were reported for randomized subjects in Study V9231—145 in the naldemedine group and 120 in the placebo group—and are summarized in Table 11 and Table 12.
L382: At least 1 major protocol deviation was reported for 182 subjects (96 subjects in the naldemedine group and 86 subjects in the placebo group). As discussed in the previous section, the difference between treatment arms is primarily due to the higher percentage of subjects in the naldemedine arm without the minimum required efficacy data. Compared to placebo, the naldemedine group had 5% more subjects without ≥4 weeks of data (i.e., mITT violation) and an additional 2.5% more subjects who withdrew before Day 81 of treatment period (PP violation), which can be attributed, in large part, to the higher rate of TEAE-related early discontinuations among naldemedine-treated subjects.

L386: ## Demographic and Baseline Characteristics
L387: Overall, the treatment arms in Study V9231 were well-balanced with respect to demographics, prior medical history, and concomitant medical conditions and treatments. Baseline disease-related characteristics were also similar between groups.
L388: ## Subject Demographics
L389: Subject demographics for the ITT population for Study V9321 are summarized in Table 13, with a breakdown of the ITT population and clinical sites by country of origin shown in Table 14.
L390: Although the mean and median age of the treatment groups were similar, there were 17% fewer subjects aged \ge 65 years old in the naldemedine treatment group than the placebo group. This is notable because this demographic group is also at risk for age-related idiopathic constipation, which may confound treatment response. In both groups, the numbers of subjects with age \ge 75 years old were small (6 in the naldemedine group and 8 in the placebo group).
L391: Notably, mean and median body mass index (BMI) of the study population were in the obese range (31.3 \text{ kg/m}^2 and 30.3 \text{ kg/m}^2, respectively), and 55% of study subjects had a BMI in the obese range. Average BMI values and BMI distribution were similar between treatment arms. The high prevalence of obesity in this population is reflective of the high overall morbidity of this patient population and is likely a good representation of the post-marketing treatment population.
L392: Of the clinical trials sites who participated in Study V9231, 70% were in the United States, and enrollment at these sites accounted for 82% of the subjects in the V9231 ITT population.

L400: ## Other Baseline Characteristics (e.g., disease characteristics, important concomitant drugs)
L401: ## • Baseline SBM Frequency
L402: Mean frequency of SBMs at baseline for both treatment arms of the V9231 ITT population was 1.3 \pm 0.7 SBM/week, ranging between 0 to 2.5 SBM/week. Both groups also had similar distribution of baseline SBM frequency (Table 15). No subjects had a weekly SBM frequency of \ge 3 SBM/week, consistent with the eligibility criteria used to establish a diagnosis of OIC. The severity of other baseline constipation-related characteristics and symptoms were also similar between groups.

L407: ## • Baseline Opioid Use
L408: Baseline opioid use for the ITT population of Study V9231 was assessed during the 14-day Screening Period. For each subject, a baseline mean total daily dose (TDD) of opioids was calculated and expressed in morphine equivalents based on standard conversion factors. Baseline opioid TDD data are shown in Table 16.
L409: The mean TDD of opioid was ~15 mg (morphine equivalents) higher for the placebo group than the naldemedine group, though the median dose was the same for both groups. This difference was driven by the higher percentage of patients receiving average doses &gt;400 mg morphine equivalents/day (5.5% vs. 3.3%). When the mean daily dose at baseline was calculated for subjects taking up to 400 mg, no difference between groups was observed.
L410: In addition, 5 of the 6 patients with mean TDD below the minimum required dose of 30 mg morphine equivalents/day were in the placebo arm. In spite of these differences, the distribution of opioid doses were similar between &gt;95% of the subjects in both 2 treatment arms, making it unlikely that these differences had a significant impact on efficacy results.

L418: ## ➤ **Other Concomitant medications**
L419: ## *Non-opioid analgesics*
L420: In addition to opioids, 115 subjects (42%) in the naldemedine group and 110 subjects (40%) in the placebo group were receiving ≥1 non-opioid pain medication (Table 18). The most class of non-opioid analgesics in both groups were GABA analogues, such as gabapentin and pregabalin, and non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs). Use of NSAIDs, a class of medications with common adverse effect of constipation, was higher in the placebo group (31.5%, vs. 27.8% in the naldemedine arm).

L424: ## *Concomitant Medications (Excluding Analgesics and Laxatives)*
L425: Classes of medications used by ≥5% of subjects in Study V9231 are shown in Table 19. Concomitant medication use was generally similar between groups. In both groups, the most common classes of concomitant medications (excluding analgesics and laxatives) were
L426: centrally acting muscle relaxants (e.g., tizanidine, baclofen, cyclobenzaprine, carisoprodol, etc.) and benzodiazepines.
L427: One notable difference is the higher rate of use of selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs), a class of medications with common side effect of constipation, among subjects in the placebo arm (21.3%, vs. 15.0% in the naldemedine arm). In the naldemedine arm, usage of beta adrenergic blockers was greater (19.7%, vs. 15.4% in placebo).

L444: Hypothyroidism was more common among placebo-treated subjects (14.7%) than naldemedine subjects (10.6%), consistent with the higher frequency of thyroid hormone replacement among subjects in the naldemedine arm. Although hypothyroidism is strongly associated with constipation, this disparity is unlikely to be a confounder because control of hypothyroidism was a prerequisite for study participation. It should be noted, however, that the same imbalance in favor of the naldemedine arm also occurred in Study V9232 (See Section 6.2.2).
L445: In the naldemedine arm, hypertension was more common (53.6%, compared to placebo 45.3%; difference 8.3%). This is an important consideration for analyses of hypertension-related TEAE and vital sign data, particularly because there is a plausible mechanism for increased blood pressure with PAMORA treatment. It is important to note, however, that the same imbalance in favor of the placebo arm occurred in Study V9232. Also of note, seasonal allergy was more frequent among naldemedine subjects, but the rate systemic antihistamine use was substantially higher among the subjects in this treatment arm.
L446: For both groups, hypertension was the 2nd most common medical condition in the ITT population (~50% of subjects), with higher rate among subjects in the naldemedine group (51.3%, vs. 47.1% of placebo subjects; difference 4.2%). On the other hand, the incidence of dyslipidemia, also in the top 5 most common medical conditions for both groups, was lower in the naldemedine group (37.7%, vs. 42.3% in the placebo group; difference -4.6%).
L447: Similarly, the incidence of two important cardiovascular conditions of interest was also lower in the naldemedine group, specifically:
L448: - Myocardial infarction in 5 subjects in the naldemedine group (1.8%) vs. 10 subjects in the placebo group (3.7%),"Myocardial ischemia" was reported for 3 additional subjects in the placebo group (0 in the naldemedine group); combined incidence of myocardial infarction/ischemia in the placebo group was 4.8%
- Coronary artery disease in 7 subjects in the naldemedine group (2.6%) vs. 18 subjects in the placebo group (6.6%)1 subject in each group had a diagnosis of "arteriosclerosis"
L449: Overall, peripheral arterial disease was rare (total 6 subjects), but the incidence was disproportionately higher in the naldemedine group [n=5 (1.8%) vs. n=1 (0.4%) in the placebo group].
L450: The group differences for these cardiac conditions and cardiovascular risk factors are important to this application due to their relevance to MACE assessments (see Section 8.5.1), but were unlikely to have impacted efficacy results.
L451: Also relevant to the safety review was the difference in respiratory conditions between groups. Overall, 98 subjects in the placebo group (36.0%) reported \ge 1 respiratory condition, vs. 81 in the naldemedine group (29.7%, difference 6.3%). Specifically, COPD, including emphysema and chronic bronchitis, was more common in the placebo group (n=32; 11.7%), compared to the naldemedine group (n=18, 6.6%). The incidence of sleep apnea was higher in the placebo group (11.0%, vs. 8.4% in the naldemedine group). The rates of other respiratory conditions, including asthma and allergic rhinitis/sinusitis, were similar between groups.
L452: In the naldemedine group, history of neck pain was more common (20.9%, vs. 15.1% in the placebo group). This finding is relevant for interpretation of AE data, in which neck pain occurred more frequently in the naldemedine group (see Section 8.4.5).
L453: In general, the incidence of mood disorders, nervous system disorders, cardiovascular disease, diabetes, renal disease, and hepatobiliary disorders were similar between groups.
L454: ## Study Compliance
L455: ## - Treatment Compliance- Study Drug
L456: Study drug compliance rates were similar between treatment groups. Mean compliance with study drug during the Treatment Period of Study V9231 was 96.7% for the naldemedine group and 98.6% for the placebo group.
L457: ## • Treatment Compliance- Study Drug Diary completion
L458: Seventy-three subjects had ≥1 week with non-evaluable efficacy data (i.e., data for &lt;4 of 7 days)—38 in the naldemedine group and 35 in the placebo group. Of these, only 7 active subjects (3 naldemedine- and 4 placebo-treated subjects) had non-evaluable data for ≥3 study weeks, suggesting overall good eDiary compliance during study participation.
L459: ## Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L460: Treatment response (i.e., “responder”) was defined as ≥9 positive-response weeks out of the 12-week Treatment Period and ≥3 positive-response weeks out of the last 4 weeks of the 12-week Treatment Period, where a positive-response week was defined as ≥3 SBMs per week and an increase from baseline of ≥1 SBM per week for that week.
L461: The proportion of responders by treatment group and the between-group comparisons for the ITT Population are summarized in Table 22. The proportion of SBM responders in the naldemedine group was 47.6% vs. 34.6% in the placebo group, for which the treatment difference (13.0%) was statistically significant (p&lt;0.002) and 95% confidence intervals for group responder rates did not overlap. (Table 21) Also, similar treatment differences were observed when treatment groups were analyzed by opioid dose strata—13.1% and 13.0% for the lower and higher dose strata, respectively (Table 22) and only a small (3%) difference in response rates was observed between opioid dose strata for both treatment arms.

L469: Results for all 6 primary endpoint sensitivity analyses were consistent with the primary analysis of the primary endpoint (ITT population). In each case, the proportion of responders in the naldemedine group was higher than in the placebo group with statistically significant differences between groups (p-value range 0.0001 to 0.0220, where the highest p-value is for the “Worst Case” analysis). These results support that pre-specified definitions for evaluable data and methods for handling missing data did not significantly impact primary endpoint analyses.
L470: Of the 6 sensitivity analyses, the “modified worst case” analysis is the one that best addresses the risk of bias due to correction of weekly BM event frequencies (i.e., adjustment based on the number of completed diary entries for the week). As shown in Table 23, the modified worst case analysis of primary endpoint data yielded very similar results to the primary (ITT population) analysis. With use of uncorrected SBM frequencies for the modified worst case analysis, the responder classification for only 1 of the 545 subjects in the ITT population differed from the primary analysis classification.
L471: Not surprisingly, the response rates for both groups are highest for the per protocol (PP) analysis population which included all subjects enrolled through Study Day 81. Therefore, all discontinuations prior to Study Day 81 were excluded from the PP population analysis, compared to the primary analysis of the ITT population, in which these subjects were classified non-responders. Because the rates of study discontinuations were similar between groups, response rates for both groups were similarly affected by missing data.
L472: However, a shorter duration of efficacy data (minimum 4 weeks) was required for inclusion in the mITT population. Because TEAE-related discontinuations in the naldemedine group tended to occur early in treatment, the largest treatment difference was measured using the mITT population (17.2% difference between groups; 95% CI 8.6 to 25.1). A similar effect was seen for the Complete Case analysis.
L473: Overall, these mITT and PP sensitivity analyses suggest results of the primary analysis may underestimate the magnitude of treatment effect of naldemedine due to the conservative methods for handling missing data. Although tolerability is an important factor in the overall benefit-risk assessment of the drug, the interpretation of efficacy data needs to take into account the impact of these dropouts on primary analyses.

L478: ## Data Quality and Integrity – Reviewers' Assessment
L479: Overall, the quality of naldemedine safety and efficacy data was adequate to conduct the necessary benefit-risk assessments for this application. Despite comments by the Applicant describing challenges faced with respect to eDiary compliance, the integrity of efficacy data from self-reported efficacy assessments did not seem to be compromised by missing eDiary data, a finding which is supported by the sensitivity analyses of the primary efficacy endpoint.
L480: For one domain, opioid medication doses, the accuracy of data collected during all phases of the study (i.e., data collected at screening, during baseline assessments, and throughout study treatment) were deemed unreliable. This reviewer's judgment was based upon findings of discrepant data between investigator (EDC)- and patient (eDiary)-reported opioid doses and unusually high variability and deviant values among individual subjects' doses. In an effort to
L481: address this problem, the Applicant conducted analyses excluding patients they identified discrepant EDC and eDiary data during the treatment period; however, this strategy for analyses was minimally helpful because the Applicant did address any discrepancies among baseline data. The limited usefulness of opioid dose data impacted a broad range of efficacy and safety analyses based on these data because opioid doses information was used both a disease-related characteristic for stratification and subgroup risk assessment, as well as a measure of opioid analgesia. Yet, despite the scope of analyses affected by these data as noted throughout this review, the overall determinations of naldemedine safety and efficacy to support marketing approval were not significantly impacted by this issue. However, this experience underscores the challenges of collecting detailed opioid usage data during clinical trials.
L482: ## Efficacy Results – Secondary endpoints
L483: Four secondary efficacy endpoints were predefined for Study V9231.
L484: - Change in the frequency of spontaneous bowel movements per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the treatment period
- Change in the frequency of spontaneous bowel movements per week from baseline to week 1 of the treatment period
- Change in the frequency of complete spontaneous bowel movements (CSBM) per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the treatment period
- Change in the Frequency of Spontaneous Bowel Movements without Straining per Week from Baseline to the Last 2 Weeks of the Treatment Period
L485: Consistent with primary endpoint analyses, the ITT population was designated as the primary analysis population for secondary endpoint analyses.
L486: For each of the 4 key secondary endpoints, the mean increase in BM event frequency from baseline to the specified analysis timepoint was greater in the naldemedine arm vs. the placebo arm. Results were statistically significant for all 4 secondary endpoints. Similar to the primary endpoint, the data analyses of the secondary endpoints provided by the Applicant were conducted using pro-rated weekly BM event frequencies. The review team also requested additional post-hoc analyses that were based on unadjusted BM event frequencies. For both unadjusted and adjusted data, secondary endpoints are summarized in Table 24.
L487: Treatment differences calculated based on adjusted data were consistently greater than those based on unadjusted data. Compared to unadjusted data, use of adjusted data resulted in inflated treatment differences between arms exclusively favoring the naldemedine group. Although the use of data adjustment is not statistically important (i.e., calculated p-values for treatment differences based on unadjusted data are &lt;0.0001 to 0.0012), results based on unadjusted data are considered less biased. This reviewer acknowledges that missing data could result in underestimation of BM event frequencies based on unadjusted data (i.e., BM event frequencies based only on observed events). Therefore, this reviewer recommends that

CDER Clinical Review Template 2015 Edition80
Version date: June 25, 2015 for initial rollout (NME/original BLA reviews)
L488: any secondary endpoint data included in mean BM event frequencies reported in the efficacy section of the prescribing information should be calculated exclusively using unadjusted data.

L497: ## Dose/Dose Response
L498: Only 1 naldemedine dosage (0.2 mg once daily) was evaluated in controlled phase 3 clinical trials, including Study V9231. Therefore, analyses of dose-response are limited to the phase 2 clinical trial (see Section 5.5.2).
L499: ## Durability of Response
L500: The responder definition for primary efficacy analyses includes a specific criterion (i.e., weekly response for 3 of the last 4 weeks of the treatment period) to incorporate a requirement for durability of treatment response as part of the overall definition of treatment response.
L501: Demonstration of sustained treatment based on weekly response rates over time is shown in Figure 10.

L507: ## Persistence of Effect
L508: The half-life of naldemedine is relatively short, and there is no evidence to suggest that treatment effects would persist after stopping the drug.
L509: ## Additional Analyses Conducted on the Individual Trial

L512: During the treatment period, 164 subjects (60.1%) in the naldemedine arm of the ITT population reported rescue medication use (\ge1 dose), compared to 204 subjects in the placebo arm (75.0%). L513: group with \ge 8 weeks of data.

L522: ## Subject Global Satisfaction (SGS) Scores
L523: A Subject Global Satisfaction (SGS) evaluation was completed at Visit 7 (Week 12)/Early Termination, by 372 subjects in the ITT population (68.3%)—185 subjects in the naldemedine group (67.8%) and 187 subjects in the placebo group(68.8%). Results are shown in Table 26.
L524: In the naldemedine group, 140 of the 185 subjects with completed assessments, 140/185 (75.7%) had scores 5-7 (i.e., markedly, moderately, or slightly improved), compared with 107/187 subjects (57.2%) in the placebo group (nominal p=0.0005).
L525: By itself, SGS information is considered exploratory in nature. These data of subject satisfaction with treatment were proposed by the Clinical Outcomes Assessment (COA) team as a possible anchor score to assess the clinical meaningfulness of secondary endpoint data. At the request of the Agency, the Applicant created cumulative distribution function (CDF) plots of secondary endpoint data and SGS scores. These figures are located in the Appendix, Section 13.4; results are discussed in Section 7.1.3.

L529: ## Changes in Total and Free Testosterone in Males
L530: The mean changes in total and free testosterone in males from baseline to Visit 7 (Week 12)/Early Termination for the naldemedine group were small and not meaningfully different from the placebo group.
L531: ## 6.2. Study V9232
L532: ## 6.2.1. Study Design
L533: ## Overview and Objective
L534: The clinical trial design of Study V9232 was the same to Study V9231. Like Study V9231, Study V9232 was a 12-week, multicenter, international clinical trial entitled "A Randomized, Double-blind, Placebo-controlled, Parallel-group Study of Naldemedine in the Treatment of Opioid-induced Constipation in Subjects with Non-malignant Chronic Pain Receiving Opioid Therapy." Refer to Section 6.1.1 for the description and discussion of the following clinical trial elements, which were the same to those reported for Study V9231:
L535: - Overview and objective
- Trial design
- Study population (i.e., target enrollment, eligibility criteria)
- Study treatments and rescue medications
- Prohibited concomitant medications and non-medication study restrictions
- Study endpoints, including the primary endpoint and all secondary and exploratory endpoints
- Study procedures and assessments
- Statistical analysis plan, including efficacy analysis population
L536: Study V9232 was conducted from 04 November 2013 (enrollment of the first subject) to 09 June 2015 (completion of the last subject).
L537: ## Protocol Amendments
L538: Two protocol amendments were applied to the original protocol for Study V9231, dated 04 October 2013 (concurrent with Amendment 1 of Study V9231), both of which were issued after initiation of enrollment on 29 August 2013.
L539: - Amendment 1: 11 June 2014
- Amendment 2: 16 October 2014
L540: The two protocol amendments for Study V9232 correspond with Amendments 2 and 3 for Study V9231, demonstrating consistency in the conduct of the 2 trials.
L541: The key changes implemented with the V9232 protocol amendments included the following:
L542: ## Amendment 1:
L543: - Added clarification regarding discontinuation of regular use of laxatives at start of screening and through the 12-week Treatment Period
- Clarification regarding stratification based on morphine-equivalent dosing
- Clarification regarding allowable use of tramadol and tapentadol
- Clarification of exclusion criteria related to severe constipation prior to and during the Screening Period
- Clarification of primary efficacy endpoint related to last observation carried forward
L544: Clinical Review
Lauren Weintraub, Clinical Reviewer
NDA 208854
Symproic (Naldemedine Tosylate)
L545: (LOCF)
L546: ## Amendment 2:
L547: - Revised secondary efficacy endpoints to provide a more thorough clinical efficacy summary of naldemedine including effects from baseline to endpoint, baseline to the first week, straining, and CSBMs
- Added an exploratory endpoint to further assess the effect on SBMs without straining over time
- Removed PK assessment as an exploratory endpoint;
- Changed the mITT Population definition to produce a population that more accurately accounted for the challenges encountered by subjects required to use an EDC tool
- Modified the Safety Population definition to be more inclusive in order to obtain a larger population
- Further clarified the definition of insufficient primary endpoint data and “nonresponse” week
L548: ## 6.2.2. Study Results
L549: ## Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L550: The Applicant reported that operating procedures and audit programs were by the CRO and Applicant in accordance with the Good Clinical Practice (GCP) guidelines, as documented by the International Conference on Harmonization (ICH) and the FDA.
L551: ## Financial Disclosure
L552: None of the site investigators who participated in this clinical trial had disclosable financial information. Additional details are provided in Section 13.2.
L553: ## Subject Disposition
L554: The subject disposition for all screened subjects in V9232 is shown in Figure 12. Of the 277 subjects randomized to naldemedine, 5 subjects were not dosed (vs. 0 in the placebo arm).
L555: - Subject 22309-001- randomized accidentally by the site (ineligible due to) and was discontinued before receiving the first dose of study drug,
- Subject 22316-016 was randomized but not dosed due to ineligibility because the patient was unable to comply with all study procedures
L556: - Subject 22324-002 withdrew consent prior to the first dose of study drug as the subject could not stay at the site for the time required to complete all study procedures required for the randomization visit
- Subject 22335-018 withdrew consent prior to dosing due to “personal reasons”
- Subject 22351-008 was randomized prematurely, and prior to dosing, was found to have a mildly tender lump in the abdomen, which, according to the Investigator, required further evaluation. The subject was discontinued prior to the first dose of study drug
L557: As shown in Figure 13, the study completion and discontinuation rates were similar between treatment groups in Study V9232. Forty (14.4%) in the naldemedine group and 44 (15.9%) in the placebo group discontinued study participation, and the reasons for discontinuation were also generally similar between groups. Sixteen subjects (5.8%) in the naldemedine arm vs 11 subjects (4.0%) in the placebo arm in the placebo arm withdrew due to a TEAE, including 2 subjects in each treatment group for whom the onset of the TEAE occurred prior to the first dose of study medication. This difference in TEAE-related discontinuation rates between V9232 treatment arms (31% difference) is notably less than Study V9231, in which study discontinuations due to TEAEs were almost 3-fold higher in the naldemedine arm as compared to the placebo arm. (Discontinuations due to TEAEs are discussed in detail in the Safety Evaluation in Section 8.)
L558: Overall, a similar proportion of subjects (naldemedine 85.6%; placebo 84.1%) in both treatment groups completed the study.

L574: ## Protocol Violations/Deviations
L575: A total of 302 major protocol deviations were reported for randomized subjects in Study V9232—148 in the naldemedine group and 154 the placebo group—and are summarized in Table 27 and Table 28.
L576: At least 1 major protocol deviation was reported for 220 subjects (103 subjects in the naldemedine group and 117 subjects in the placebo group). Unlike Study V9232, the percentage of subjects without the minimum required efficacy data for inclusion in both the mITT and PP populations were similar between groups. The most commonly reported protocol deviation was subject withdrawal before Day 81 of the Treatment Period: 37 (13.4%) subjects in the naldemedine group and 42 (15.2%) subjects in the placebo group.

L580: ## Demographic and Baseline Characteristics
L581: Overall, the treatment arms in Study V9232 were well-balanced with respect to demographics, prior medical history, and concomitant medical conditions and treatments. Baseline disease-related characteristics were also similar between groups.
L582: ## Subject Demographics
L583: Subject demographics for the ITT population for Study V9232 are summarized in Table 29, with a breakdown of the ITT population and clinical sites by country of origin shown, along with Study V9231 data, in Section 7.1.2 (Table 14). The Study V9232 study population had a similar imbalance in age distribution between treatment arms as Study V9231, with a higher proportion of subjects between ages 40 and 65 years old. In addition, both treatment arms in Study V9232 had small numbers of subjects aged \ge 75 years old (9 subjects in the naldemedine group and 5 subjects in the placebo group).
L584: Also similar to Study V9231, mean and median body mass index (BMI) of the V9232 study population were in the obese range (31.3 \text{ kg/m}^2 and 30.3 \text{ kg/m}^2, respectively), and &gt;50% of study subjects had a BMI in the obese range. Average BMI values were similar between treatment arms, although there were fewer subjects in the naldemedine arm with BMI in the normal range compared to placebo (16.3% vs. 19.3%, respectively) and more subjects in the overweight range (30.4% vs. 26.6%, respectively). The high prevalence of obesity in this population is reflective of the high overall morbidity of this patient population and is likely a good representation of the post-marketing treatment population.
L585: Of the clinical trials sites who participated in Study V9232, &gt;75% were in the United States, and enrollment at these sites accounted for 87% of the subjects in the V9232 ITT population.

L591: ## Other Baseline Characteristics (e.g., disease characteristics, important concomitant drugs)
L592: ## • Baseline SBM Frequency
L593: Mean frequency of SBMs at baseline for both treatment arms of the V9232 ITT population was 1.2 SBM/week (range 0 to 2.5 SBM/week). Both groups also had similar distribution of baseline SBM frequency (Table 30). No subjects had a weekly SBM frequency of \ge 3 SBM/week, consistent with the eligibility criteria used to establish a diagnosis of OIC. The severity of other baseline constipation-related characteristics and symptoms were also similar between groups.

L599: ## • **Baseline Opioid Use**
L600: Baseline opioid use for the ITT population of Study V9232 was assessed during the 14-day Screening Period. L612: ## • Other Concomitant Medications
L613: ## *Non-opioid analgesics*
L614: In addition to opioids, 151 subjects in the naldemedine group (55%) and 170 subjects in the placebo group (65%) were receiving ≥1 non-opioid pain medication (Table 33). The most classes of non-opioid analgesics in both groups were NSAIDs and GABA analogues, such as gabapentin, pregabalin, and gabapentin. NSAID use was similar between treatment arms in Study V9232, but use of GABA analogues higher in the placebo group (38.3%, vs. 29.7% in the naldemedine arm).

L619: ## *Concomitant Medications (Excluding Analgesics and Laxatives)*
L620: Classes of medications used by \ge 25\% of the ITT population in Study V9232 are shown in Table 34. Concomitant medication use was generally similar between groups. Centrally acting muscle relaxants comprised the most common class of concomitant medications (excluding analgesics and laxatives) in both treatment arms (&gt;1/3 of subjects in each arm). Other potentially constipating medications with higher rate in the placebo arm are multivitamins
L621: This reviewer has identified some differences between groups, notably use of both serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) and SSRIs, antidepressants with common side effect of constipation, were both lower in the naldemedine arm, with rates 13.4% and 17.4% in the naldemedine arm vs. 19.0% and 20.4% in the placebo arm, for SNRIs and SSRIs, respectively. Use of thyroid hormone was higher in the placebo group and is a reflection of the higher rate of hypothyroidism among those subjects. On the hand, rates of use for beta-blockers, alpha-blockers, and sedative-hypnotic sleep agents (e.g., zolpidem, zaleplon, eszopiclone, etc.), other medications associated with constipation, were higher in the naldemedine arm.

L631: ## *Medical History: General*
L632: The most common medical conditions among subjects in Study V9232, occurring in &gt;1/3 of ITT population of both treatment arms, were back pain (also the most commonly reported cause of subjects' non-cancer pain), hypertension, depression, dyslipidemia, anxiety, and insomnia. These results are similar to Study V9231. All medical conditions reported in at least 10% of the ITT population are listed in Table 36.
L633: Of note, GERD was more common in the placebo arm (relevant for abdominal pain assessments), as well as hypothyroidism, which is strongly associated with constipation but should not have been a confounding factor because thyroid control was a prerequisite for study for study participation. In the placebo arm, anxiety and depression were also more common than in the naldemedine, which is consistent with the higher rate of SSRI and SNRI use in that treatment arm.
L634: In the naldemedine arm, the prevalence of hypertension was higher than the placebo arm. This is an important consideration for interpretation of hypertension-related TEAEs, particularly because there is a plausible mechanism for elevated blood pressure with naldemedine and other PAMORAs.

L639: ## Study Compliance
L640: ## - **Treatment Compliance- Study Drug**
L641: Study drug compliance rates were similar between treatment groups. Mean compliance with study drug during the Treatment Period was 95.86% for the naldemedine group and 96.42% for the placebo group.
L642: ## - **Treatment Compliance- Study Drug Diary completion**
L643: Seventy-eight subjects had ≥1 week with non-evaluable efficacy data (i.e., data for &lt;4 of 7 days)—36 in the naldemedine group and 42 in the placebo group. Only 2 active subjects (both in the naldemedine group) had non-evaluable data for ≥3 study weeks, suggesting overall good eDiary compliance during study participation.
L644: ## Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L645: ## Proportion of Spontaneous Bowel Movement (SBM) Responders
L646: Responder definitions in Study V9232 were the same as those used in Study V9231 (See Section 6.1.2).
L647: The proportion of responders by treatment group and the between-group comparisons for the ITT Population are summarized in Table 37. The proportion of SBM responders in the naldemedine group was 52.5% vs. 33.6% in the placebo group (+18.9%, p&lt;0.0001), a statistically
L648: significant difference between the 2 groups. Similar to study V9231, these results are statistically significant, and these results are even more robust than those from Study V9231.

L655: Results for all 6 primary endpoint sensitivity analyses were consistent with the primary (ITT population) analysis of the primary endpoint. In each case, the proportion of responders in the naldemedine group was higher than in the placebo group with statistically significant differences between groups (p-value range &lt;0.0001 to 0.0007, where the highest p-value is for the "Worst Case" analysis.) These results support that pre-specified definitions for evaluable data and methods for handling missing data did not significantly impact the interpretability/reliability of primary endpoint data.
L656: Of the 6 sensitivity analyses, the "modified worst case" analysis is the one that best addresses the risk of bias due to correction of weekly BM event frequencies (i.e., adjustment based on the number of completed diary entries for the week). As shown in Table 39, the modified worst case analysis of primary endpoint data yielded very similar results to the primary (ITT population) analysis. With use of uncorrected SBM frequencies for the modified worst case analysis, the responder classification for only 1 of the 545 subjects in the ITT population differed from the primary analysis classification.
L657: As predicted by the similar exclusion rate for the mITT population, the treatment effect was not substantially higher for the mITT population, compared to the primary analysis, as was observed for Study V9231. Although TEAE-related discontinuations still occurred relatively
L658: early during the treatment period, the overall dropout rate during the 1st 4 weeks was more similar in this study. However, as expected, the individual group response rates are highest among the mITT and PP populations for Study V9232.

L662: ## Data Quality and Integrity – Reviewers' Assessment
L663: Similar discrepancies were identified in opioid dose data of Study V9232 as Study V9231. Refer to Section 7.1.2 for discussion of these data integrity concerns and their potential impact on the interpretation of study data.
L664: ## Efficacy Results – Secondary endpoints
L665: The same 4 secondary endpoints were pre-specified for both Study V9231 and Study V9232:
L666: - Change in the frequency of spontaneous bowel movements per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the treatment period
L667: - Change in the frequency of spontaneous bowel movements per week from baseline to week 1 of the treatment period
- Change in the frequency of complete spontaneous bowel movements (CSBM) per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the treatment period
- Change in the Frequency of Spontaneous Bowel Movements without Straining per Week from Baseline to the Last 2 Weeks of the Treatment Period
L668: The ITT population was the primary analysis population used for secondary endpoint analyses.
L669: Like Study V9231, treatment differences between the naldemedine and placebo arms were statistically significant for all 4 key secondary endpoints (p-values &lt;0.0001 to 0.0011), i.e., the naldemedine group had statistically greater increases in frequency of SBM per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the treatment period, SBM per week from baseline to Week 1 of treatment, CSBM per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the treatment period, and SBM without straining per week from baseline to the last 2 weeks of the treatment period, compared to the placebo group.
L670: As pre-specified in the statistical analysis plan, the frequencies of BM events per week used for secondary endpoint analyses were adjusted based on the number of diary entries completed for the particular week. However, this methodology raises concern for potential responder bias. Therefore, the Applicant conducted post-hoc analyses using unadjusted BM frequencies (i.e., only observed BM events per week) at Agency's request. A comparison of results from adjusted and unadjusted data are shown in
L671: Statistically, these re-analyses were consistent with the original analyses (p-values &lt;0.0001 to 0.0014), thus demonstrating the overall robustness of these secondary efficacy data. However, treatment differences between groups were consistently smaller using unadjusted data compared to analyses with adjusted data, albeit less than those observed in Study V9231.
L672: A summary of secondary endpoint analyses for both adjusted and unadjusted BM frequencies are presented in Table 40.

L709: ## 6.3. Study V9235
L710: ## 6.3.1. Study Design
L711: *Introductory Comment: Study V9235 was conducted primarily to assess the long-term safety of naldemedine. Evaluation of efficacy was a secondary objective, and therefore, the efficacy data from Study V9235 are viewed as supportive. Furthermore, the trial design of Study V9235 introduced potential confounders of efficacy data interpretation, particularly with respect to allowable concomitant medications and rescue laxative use that were not included in Studies V9231 and V9232. The broader range of medications permitted during participation in this 52-*
L712: Clinical Review
Lauren Weintraub, Clinical Reviewer
NDA 208854
Symproic (Naldemedine Tosylate)
L713: *week safety trial is understandable in an effort to maintain enrollment in long-term placebo-blinded studies.*
L714: ## Overview and Objective
L715: Title: A Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Parallel-Group, Multicenter, Phase 3 Study to Evaluate the Long-Term Safety of Naldemedine for the Treatment of Opioid-Induced Constipation in Subjects with Non-Malignant Chronic Pain Receiving Opioid Therapy
L716: Study V9235 was designed primarily as a long-term safety trial. Although the Applicant included efficacy assessments, this study did not have any primary efficacy objectives.
L717: ## Trial Design
L718: Title: A Randomized, Double-blind, Placebo-controlled, Parallel-group, Multicenter, Phase 3 Study to Evaluate the Long-Term Safety of Naldemedine for the Treatment of Opioid-Induced Constipation in Subjects with Non-Malignant Chronic Pain Receiving Opioid Therapy
L719: The primary objective of study V9235 was to assess the overall safety and tolerability of naldemedine in subjects with non-malignant chronic pain receiving a stable opioid therapy regimen for \ge 1 month and having opioid-induced constipation (OIC).
L720: ## Study Population
L721: Approximately 1200 subjects were to be randomized in a 1:1 ratio to either the naldemedine or placebo treatment arm (600 subjects each).
L722: The original target study population was 1500 subjects but was reduced to 1200 subjects.
L723: **Reviewer Comment:** For Study V9235, the data analyses for NDA submission (both safety and efficacy) were conducted using data from the “Core Period Data Set” (data cut-off date 24 June 2015). As of this data cut-off date, 747/1241 subjects in the ITT population (60.2%) had reached study completion/termination (376 in the naldemedine arm and 369 in the placebo arm). For the 496 subjects with ongoing study participation at the end of the Core Period, all had completed
L724: *Efficacy analyses were conducted using data from the Core Period.*
L725: ## Study Eligibility
L726: Subject eligibility requirements with respect to OIC-related baseline characteristics and disease severity were the same as Studies V9231 and V9232. Subjects must have had self-reported OIC a and a documented medical history indicating non-malignant chronic pain for at least 3 months' duration and a stable opioid regimen with an average total daily dose of \ge 30 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate for at least 1 month prior to the Screening Visit.
L727: Like Studies V9231 and V9232, subject eligibility and SBM status (i.e., frequency of SBMs) were based on a 14-consecutive-day qualifying period during the 28-day Screening Period. Subjects must have recorded at least 4 days of BM data for each 7-day period that constituted 1 week in the electronic diary (eDiary). Subjects who did not meet the eligibility criteria during the first 14 days of the Screening Period may have continued for up to 28 days to allow them to meet the eligibility criteria. No more than 4 SBMs total over the 14-consecutive-day qualifying period were allowed. In addition, no more than 3 SBMs in a given week of the qualifying period were allowed.
L728: Subjects were not required to be on a stable laxative regimen to be eligible for participation in study V9235. Subjects with a stable laxative regimen prior to enrollment were permitted to stay on their regimen for the duration of the study.
L729: With the exception of laxative medication use, inclusion and exclusion criteria for Study V9235 were the same as Studies V9231 and V9232.
L730: ## ***Study Treatment***
L731: Subjects were randomized in a 1:1 ratio to receive a single tablet of either 0.2 mg of naldemedine or placebo (600 subjects each) QD in a double-blind manner for 52 weeks. At the time of randomization, subjects were stratified based on their documented opioid use (average TDD during the 14-consecutive-day qualifying period) as follows:
L732: - 30 to 100 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate (average TDD)
- 100 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate (average TDD)
L733: The naldemedine formulation used in Study V9325 was the same as used in other Phase 3 clinical trials, a tablet for oral administration, containing 0.2 mg of the active compound. Placebo tablets were identical to active tablets in appearance, taste, color, and odor. The study drug (and placebo) was dispensed using the same procedures as Studies V9231 and V9232.
L734: ## **Treatment Compliance:**
L735: Assessments of treatment compliance by the Investigator and/or site personnel were performed during each study visit, including:
L736: - Tablet counts performed by qualified site personnel to verify that the correct number of doses were taken based on the actual number of days that had elapsed between clinic visits
- Information provided to the Investigator/staff by the subject during Study Visits
- eDiary records (only collected for the first 14 days of the Treatment Period)
L737: If noncompliance with study medication was identified, site personnel were supposed to counsel subjects, and if non-compliance persisted (dosing compliance &lt;80%), the subject was to be withdrawn from the study. In the event that a subject was withdrawn for compliance reasons, the Investigator was responsible for notification of the Application or designee and to CDER Clinical Review Template 2015 Edition
L738: *Version date: June 25, 2015 for initial rollout (NME/original BLA reviews)*
L739: Clinical Review
Lauren Weintraub, Clinical Reviewer
NDA 208854
Symproic (Naldemedine Tosylate)
L740: make every effort to complete the Early Termination assessments and the Early Termination/Study Completion eCRF. All subject discontinuations were supposed to be registered in the IVRS/IWRS system.
L741: ## ***Prior and Concomitant Therapy***
L742: All medications (prescription and over-the-counter) taken by the subject for 3 months prior to Screening, and concomitant medications taken with the study drug through the end of the study were recorded in the CRF.
L743: ## **Concomitant Opioid Therapy**
L744: By definition of the study population, all subjects enrolled in this study were receiving opioid therapy for chronic, non-cancer pain. To be eligible for study participation, a subject was required to have been treated with opioids for \ge 3 months, with a stable opioid regimen at a TDD on average of \ge 30 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate for \ge 1 month prior to Screening and without anticipated changes in their overall opioid regimen during study participation. Opioid therapy was not provided as part of the study, as subjects were required to be on a stable opioid regimen prior to the Screening Visit.
L745: Subjects were permitted to take additional medication (opioid or non-opioid) for breakthrough pain as prescribed by their physician. At the screening visit, all opioid medication(s) taken by the subject during the prior 3 months were recorded on the eCRF. All pain medication(s) (opioid or non-opioid) were recorded by the subject throughout the study.
L746: Use of tramadol and tapentadol were permitted in this study. Tramadol and tapentadol have a dual mechanism of action, including opioid-mediated analgesia. However, these medications are considered weak opioids and are associated with less constipation than other opioids. Therefore, to ensure that subjects were receiving an adequate opioid dose, tramadol and tapentadol were not included in the calculations for either the stable opioid regimen at a TDD on average of \ge 30 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate for eligibility assessment, or stratification by average TDD.
L747: ## **Laxative Therapy**
L748: Unlike Study V9231 and Study V9232, discontinuation of laxatives was not an eligibility criterion for Study V9235. However, subjects were required to be on a stable laxative regimen prior to study enrollment. To be eligible for study participation, the subject was required to meet the OIC diagnostic criteria defined by SBM frequency, regardless of laxative use.
L749: For subjects who were not taking laxatives regularly at Screening, the laxative regimen for the 28 days prior to Screening was used as the “established laxative regimen.” To avoid fluctuations in OIC severity, subjects were supposed to maintain their established regimen without dosage changes throughout the Screening Period and Treatment Period. Any BMs that
L750: occurred on the established laxative regimen were counted as SBMs. For subjects without regular laxative use at Screening, laxative use during the Screening and Treatment Periods was documented as rescue laxative treatment.
L751: Subjects were allowed to use a rescue laxative after 72 hours without a BM. In this situation, subjects with established laxative regimens used medications/dosages that were different from their regular regimen. Subjects who were taking laxatives regularly at Screening were instructed to re-establish their baseline regimen as soon as possible after rescue laxative use. Any BM that occurred within 24 hours of rescue laxative use was not counted as an SBM. If subjects did not have a BM for 5 days, they were instructed to call the clinical site Investigator.
L752: During the Screening Period, use of rescue laxative was recorded in the eDiary; during the Treatment Period, subjects' laxative use was reviewed at each study visit and documented in the eCRF by study site personnel. Unlike Study V9231 and V9232, subjects did not maintain regular diary records of rescue laxative use.
L753: It is important to note that rescue medication use during the Treatment Period was not recorded. Therefore, SBM frequency was only used as a baseline assessment to establish study eligibility. For efficacy assessments, BM frequency (i.e., number of BM events during the specified 7-day period, irrespective of rescue laxative use) was used as the outcome variable.
L754: ***Reviewer Comment:*** *The Applicant permitted concomitant use of maintenance laxatives during this study with the expectation that subject would maintain these regimens throughout study participation. Study V9235 was primarily a long-term safety study whose success depended on the ability to compile a safety database with adequate study drug exposure, which can be particularly challenging for long-term placebo-controlled trials. Loosening of concomitant medication restrictions to allow maintenance laxative use likely helped facilitate recruitment and retention of study subjects. However, interpretation of efficacy assessments is limited by this study design choice.*
L755: *For 18% of the study population, concomitant laxative use did not comply with the procedures and definitions described above. Study data of laxative use will be discussed in more detail in Section 6.3.2. The purpose of this review comment is to convey that the procedures described above do not accurately reflect actual concomitant laxative use during the conduct of the study. Subjects with changes in maintenance laxative use from baseline were not excluded from study participation or analysis datasets. As a safety trial, no mITT or PP datasets were defined for Study V9235. In retrospect, the expectation that subjects maintain the same laxative regimen for the entire treatment duration may not have been reasonable. However, these deviations from study procedures present a major confounder for efficacy analyses, and the Applicant did not follow pre-specified or consistent procedures for handling these subjects' disposition and study data.*
L756: *To address the issue of concomitant laxative use, the Applicant defined 3 post-hoc analysis datasets (no laxative, stable, and "other") based on subjects' baseline laxative regimen and concomitant laxative use study participation, whereby the "other" subgroup was comprised of subjects who did not meet criteria for either of the other 2 subgroup, and is heterogeneous with respect to both subjects' baseline laxative regimens and the types of changes made to laxative use during study participation.*
L757: ## Prohibited Concomitant Medications (same as Study V9231 and Study V9232)
L758: Prohibited medications were not to be taken by subjects at any time during the study. Use of prohibited concomitant therapy could have resulted in study discontinuation. Prohibited medications included but were not limited to the following:
L759: - Opioid antagonists (alvimopan, methylnaltrexone bromide)
- Acetylcholine agonists (bethanechol)
- Guanylate cyclase-C agonist (linaclotide)
- 5-HT-4 agonists (prucalopride)
- Prostaglandin (misoprostol)
- Mu receptor partial agonist opioids (meptazinol)
- Mixed agonist/antagonist (nalorphine-like agonist/antagonist opioids) (pentazocine, buprenorphine)
- Antispasmodics (dicyclomine)
- Antidiarrheals (loperamide)
- Prokinetics (metoclopramide)
- Chloride channel activator (lubiprostone)
L760: Any additional medications that could have had a significant impact on the GI system or bowel habits may have been prohibited at the Investigator's discretion.
L761: ## Other Restrictions
L762: Subjects were not to initiate any new diets, supplement changes, or exercise regimens after Screening and throughout the study.
L763: ## **Study Endpoints**
L764: The efficacy measures in Study V9235 were specified as secondary and exploratory endpoints.
L765: ## ➤ Secondary Endpoints:
L766: - Change in the frequency of BMs from baseline to selected time points of the Treatment Period
- Number of subjects with laxative use
- Change in the Patient Assessment of Constipation Symptoms (PAC-SYM) Overall Score from baseline to each visit
- Change in the PAC-QOL Overall Score from baseline to each visit
L767: - Frequency of Subject Global Satisfaction by treatment group
- Exploratory endpoint: total and free testosterone levels in male subjects
L768: Of the secondary efficacy endpoints specified for Study V9235, only change in the frequency of BMs from baseline to selected time points of the Treatment Period is aligned/ comparable with the primary and major secondary endpoints of Studies V9231 and V9232. In addition, analyses of laxative use in Study V9235 are not comparable to rescue medication data from Studies V9231 and V9232 due to differences in study design which allowed concomitant use of laxatives, even if regimens were not stable prior to enrollment.
L769: **Reviewer Comment:** *In Study V9235, rescue medication data was recorded by clinical trial site personnel at study visits and used only as an independent efficacy variable. Data regarding the timing of rescue medications with respect to BM events to determine SBM frequency was only captured during the Screening Period to determine eligibility based on OIC diagnostic criteria. The outcome variable used for efficacy analysis was BM frequency, which included all BM events, irrespective of rescue laxative use. However, inclusion of BM events that required the aid of rescue medication confounds assessments of the effectiveness of treatment and need to be interpreted cautiously.*
L770: ## Study Procedures
L771: The schedule of procedures for Study V9235 is shown in Table 43.
L772: In Study V9235, an eDiary with procedures similar to those used in Studies V9231 and V9232 was used only during the Screening Period. During the Treatment Period, a “Bowel habits” patient (paper) diary was used for the pre-specified 7-day efficacy assessment periods prior to Weeks 12, 24, 36, and 52. Opioid and laxative use data were captured by study personnel during study visits.

L776: ## Statistical Analysis Plan
L777: ## Change in Frequency of BMs per Week
L778: Efficacy endpoints were the secondary endpoints of the study. For comparison between treatment groups, the significance level of tests was set at 0.05 (2-sided).
L779: The change in the frequency of BMs from baseline to selected time points of the Treatment Period was summarized using the same definition for frequency of BMs per week (derived from the number of reported BMs, adjusted for the number of eDiary entries with defecation information) as used in Studies V9231 and V9232.
L780: Using this definition, the change in the frequency of BMs per week from baseline to each selected visit [(Frequency of BMs per week for each selected visit) – (Frequency of BMs per week in baseline period)] was calculated.
L781: The mean change in frequency of BMs per week from baseline to each selected visit was compared between naldemedine and placebo using a mixed-effects repeated measures (MMRM) method which included the opioid dose strata as a covariate and treatment group, time, and time-by-treatment group interaction as fixed effects. Plots of least-square means of the changes (and their standard error) over time were presented by treatment group.
L782: ## Number of Subjects with Laxative Use
L783: Descriptive statistics of laxative use during study participation (i.e., number and proportion of subjects) were summarized with respect to baseline laxative regimen. Rescue laxative was defined as any laxative that subjects started to take during the Treatment Period.
L784: ## PAC-SYM and PAC-QOL scores
L785: The change in PAC-SYM and PAC-QOL scores (mean overall score and scores for individual domain) from baseline to each visit was calculated and summarized for each treatment group.
L786: ## Frequency of Subject Global Satisfaction
L787: Subject global satisfaction was graded at Visit 13 or early termination using the following 7-point grading scale:
L788: - 1 = markedly worsened
- 2 = moderately worsened
- 3 = slightly worsened
- 4 = unchanged
- 5 = slightly improved
- 7 = markedly improved
L789: Subject global satisfaction grades were tabulated by treatment group. The proportion was also summarized by treatment group and compared between naldemedine and placebo using the Wilcoxon rank sum test.
L790: ## Subgroup Analyses
L791: Subgroup analyses for the secondary endpoints were performed for the subgroups not on a stable laxative regimen, defined as subjects who did not have a laxative from the 28 days prior to the Screening Period to the final dose of study drug or who received only laxative as rescue at least once, and the subgroup on a stable laxative regimen.
L792: *Reviewer's Comment: As previously noted above, BM frequency (i.e., number of BM events,*
L793: *irrespective of rescue laxative use) was the only type of BM-related outcome variable used for efficacy analyses in Study V9235. SBM frequency was not used because data regarding the use and timing of rescue laxatives with respect to BM events was not collected. In Study V9235, on-treatment diary records were only required during the 4 week-long efficacy assessment periods and did not distinguish BM events based on whether rescue laxative use was required for passage. While this reviewer acknowledges that the daily eDiary requirements used in the 2 shorter clinical trials would have been too demanding for the full duration this long-term trial, treatment effect cannot be adequately assessed based BM event frequency that does not account for rescue medication use.*
L794: ## Protocol Amendments
L795: Three protocol amendments were applied to the original protocol for Study V9235, dated 09 July 2013.
L796: - Amendment 1: 07 October 2013
- Amendment 2: 16 June 2014
- Amendment 3: 26 February 2015
L797: The key changes implemented with the V9235 protocol amendments included the following:
L798: ## Amendment 1
L799: - Added a serum pregnancy test at Visit 2 (Day 1) prior to dosing for all females of childbearing potential
- Added directions for collecting data for laxative use during the Treatment Period at study site visits and recording the data on the CRF
- Added directions for recording all opioid pain medication(s) dosing daily in the eDiary during the Screening Period and for the first 2 weeks of the Treatment Period
- Added directions that opioid therapy was then to be captured on the CRF throughout the entire duration of the study
- Added list of prohibited medications that were not to be taken by subjects at any time during the study
- Specified that the Visit 8 (Week 24) safety data would be analyzed by the DSMB.
L800: ## Amendment 2
L801: - Added text to the synopsis and inclusion criteria stating that subjects must meet the following criteria based on an eDiary to be completed over a 14-consecutive-day qualifying period during the 28-day Screening Period: No more than 4 SBMs total over the 14-consecutive-day qualifying period. In addition, no more than 3 SBMs in a given week of the qualifying period.
L802: - Added text to Section 3.1 to specify that subjects might use laxatives during the Screening Period.
- Added text to specify that tramadol and tapentadol would not be used in the calculations for either inclusion criterion #3, calculation of the stable opioid regimen at a total average daily dose of \ge 30 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate, or for stratification by average daily dose of 30 to 100 mg equivalents of oral morphine sulfate versus &gt;100 mg equivalents of oral morphine.
- Specified that use of tramadol and tapentadol was allowed in the trial but only in conjunction with other opioid agonists
- Added subheading 7.7.1.1 stating that the change in frequency of BMs from baseline to selected time points in the Treatment Period was to be assessed as an efficacy endpoint. Subjects were to monitor BMs during the 52-week Treatment Period and will be asked to complete the Bowel Habits paper diary. Also, during the Treatment Period, subjects were to record BM data on the paper diary during the week prior to each of the selected study visits (ie, Bowel Habits paper diary should have been filled out during the Weeks of 11, 23, 35, and 51, corresponding to the week prior to Study Visits 6, 8, 10 and 13, respectively).
L803: ## Amendment 3
L804: - Wording of the primary objective was changed to “Assess the overall safety and tolerability of naldemedine in subjects with non-malignant chronic pain receiving a stable opioid therapy regimen for \ge 1 month and having OIC.”
- The sample size was changed as follows: “Approximately 1200 subjects will be randomized in a 1:1 ratio to receive a single tablet of either 0.2 mg of naldemedine or placebo (600 subjects each) QD in a double-blind manner during the 52-week Treatment Period.” This change was made per agreement with the US FDA and per alignment with ICH Guidance E1, The Extent of Population Exposure to Assess Clinical Safety for Drugs Intended for Long-term Treatment of Non-Life-Threatening Conditions. Exposures of at least 300-600 subjects for 6 months and 100 subjects for 1 year in each of the study arms is *[sic]* anticipated.”
- The following text was added to Section 3.1 Overall Study Design and Plan:“For analysis purposes, data from the Treatment Period will be divided into a Core Period Data Set and a Supplemental Period data set. The Core Period Data Set will consist of all subjects with at least 24 weeks of treatment (i.e., up to and including Visit 8, Week 24 at a minimum). Some subjects in the Core Period Data Set will have greater than 24 weeks of treatment (i.e., up to 12 months). The Supplemental Period data set will consist of all remaining data (i.e., study visits not completed at the time of the Core Period data lock).
L805: - The separation of the Treatment Period into the Core Period Data Set and the Supplemental Period data set does not impact study conduct or subjects. Study visits and the Schedule of Events remain as presented (Appendix 1)."
- Section 5.5 Blinding was changed to read as follows: "All subjects, the Investigator, all study personnel, and data analysts will be blinded to the treatment assigned at randomization until database lock. Treatment assignments for the [study medication] will be concealed by the use of identical packaging, labeling, dosing instructions, appearance, taste, color, and odor."
- The following text was added to Section 5.5.1 "Unblinding: Following the database lock for the Core Period, the Sponsor will be unblinded to the randomization codes. All subjects, the Investigator, and study site personnel will remain blinded.
- In Section 7.7, "(Efficacy) Assessments" was changed to "Secondary (Efficacy) Assessments".
- Section 8.4.3 Early Termination was changed to specify that subjects who terminated early should also have a Follow-up Period Visit (Visit 14; Section 8.4.2), 2 weeks after their last dose of study drug.
- The following wording was added to Section 9.1 General Considerations:"The Core Period Data Set will consist of all subjects with at least 24 weeks of treatment (ie, up to and including Visit 8, Week 24 at a minimum). Some subjects included in this data set will have completed more than 24 weeks of treatment. The Core Period Data Set is considered a complete and "stand alone" data set that will be used for Global Registration purposes. The Supplemental Period data set will consist of all remaining data (i.e., study visits not completed at the time of the Core Period data lock). For Statistical purposes, a Combined data set will also be compiled that will consist of the Core Period and the Supplemental Period."
- In Section 9.9.1 Primary Endpoint, the primary endpoint was changed from the number of Major Cardiovascular Events (MACE) during a 52-week Treatment Period to "Safety: The incidence of TEAEs, SAEs, and AEs leading to discontinuation. AEs will be classified by system organ class and preferred term using Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) Version 16.0. TEAEs will be used for safety analyses. The definition of TEAE is an AE which occurs from first dose of study drug until 14 days after last study dose."
- Section 9.9.4 Adverse Events of Special Interest was added to the protocol:"Section 9.9.4.1 Major Adverse Cardiovascular Events (MACE): The number and percentage of subjects with Major Adverse Cardiovascular Events (MACE) will be summarized by treatment group. Incidence rate of MACE will also be presented. These events will be summarized by the stratified opioid dose groups and treatment group. Major Adverse Cardiovascular Events were defined as cardiovascular death, myocardial infarction, and cerebrovascular accident.
L806: – Section 9.9.4.2 Assessment of Opioid Withdrawal: Identification of possible opioid withdrawal events will come from multiple sources, including: AE terms (e.g. DSM-V criteria and SMQ terms), Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale (COWS), Subjective Opiate Withdrawal Scale (SOWS), and Pain assessment [11-Point Numeric Rating Scale, opioid dose]. The number and the percentage of subjects with possible opioid withdrawal based on AE terms will be summarized by treatment group. Summary statistics for continuous variables such as COWS will be presented for each scheduled time point and for change from baseline to each time point."
L807: ## 6.3.2. Study Results
L808: ## Compliance with Good Clinical Practices
L809: The Applicant reported that operating procedures and audit programs were by the CRO and Applicant in accordance with the Good Clinical Practice (GCP) guidelines, as documented by the International Conference on Harmonization (ICH) and the FDA.
L810: ## Financial Disclosure
L811: None of the site investigators who participated in this clinical trial had disclosable financial information. Additional details are provided in Section 13.2.
L812: ## Subject Disposition
L813: The subject disposition for all enrolled and screened subjects as of the data cutoff date for NDA submission (the Core Data Set) is shown in Figure 17. The dropout rates for the 2 treatment arms are similar (31.1% for the naldemedine arm vs. 30.7% for the placebo arm). Of note, the observed dropout rate is similar to rates observed in other long-term trials for OIC, as noted by the Division during the Type C meeting in October 2014, held to discuss the trial design of Study V9235.
L814: Five randomized subjects in Study V9235—2 subjects randomized to the naldemedine arm (Subject IDs 52341-001 and 52344-007) and 3 subjects randomized to the placebo arm (Subject IDs 52311-004, 52344-005, and 52357-001) were excluded from the V9235 ITT and safety populations because they were also enrolled in another naldemedine clinical trial.
L815: All efficacy analyses submitted for Study V9231 were performed using the Core Period Dataset. Unlike safety analyses, efficacy analyses were not conducted using data accrued during the Supplemental Period. However, the Applicant continued to conduct planned efficacy assessments during the Supplemental Period, and the additional efficacy data accrued during this period were submitted as part of the Supplemental Period datasets.

L837: ## Demographic and Baseline Characteristics
L838: ## Demographics
L839: The demographics for the ITT population of Study V9235 are summarized in Table 46. Although the mean and median ages for the populations are similar, the number of subjects ≥65 years old and ≥75 years old is higher in the naldemedine population. In addition, the naldemedine group also included a slightly higher percentage of male subjects. Similar to the other naldemedine trials, the mean BMI for both groups was in the obese range, and &gt;50% of subjects in both groups were obese. Also similar to other trials, the majority of subjects in Study V9235 were white.

L843: ## Other Baseline Disease Characteristics
L844: ## ➤ SBM Frequency
L845: Mean SBM frequency at baseline was the same between treatment arms (Table 47). Twelve subjects in the ITT population, 7 subjects the naldemedine arm and 5 subjects in the placebo arm, had baseline SBM frequency \ge 3 per week, which exceeds the upper limit specified for study eligibility.
L846: In the placebo arm, baseline SBM frequency was higher in the subjects without baseline laxative use (Table 48). This group also had the lowest proportion of subjects with &lt;1 SBM/week at baseline.
