L862: ## ➤ Baseline Laxative Use
L863: Unlike Studies V9231 and V9232, concomitant regular laxative use was permitted in Study V9235 if subjects were on a stable laxative regimen for at least 28 days prior to screening. A stable laxative regimen was defined as regular use of ≥1 laxative at least once weekly. Subjects on a stable laxative regimen at Screening were to remain on that regimen throughout the study. Any laxative use other than those designated as the subject's stable laxative regimen was considered rescue medication.
L864: Regardless of being on a stable laxative regimen at baseline, if a subject had not had a BM for more than 72 hours at any point during the Screening or Treatment Periods, a rescue laxative (different from the subject's regular regimen for patients on a regular laxative regimen) was allowed. Subjects on stable laxatives at Screening were supposed to re-establish their baseline regimen as soon as possible following rescue medication use.
L865: Randomization of subjects was not stratified by baseline laxative use. However, the proportion of subjects on a baseline stable laxative regimen was similar between groups. A total of 831 subjects (67.0 %) in the ITT population [411 in the naldemedine group (66.2 %) and 420 in the placebo group (67.7%)] were on a stable baseline laxative regimen. For both treatment arms, mean number of laxatives was 1.3 medications per subject, and median numbers of laxatives was 1.0 (range 1 to 4) medications. The types of laxatives in subjects' baseline regimens were generally similar between treatment arms (see Table 51 and Table 52). In both arms, stimulant laxatives were the most frequent class of laxatives used by subjects on stable regimens at baseline. Use of osmotic laxatives was somewhat higher in the naldemedine arm.
L866: Among subjects in the higher opioid dose group (i.e., &gt;100 mg), 72.6% of subjects were on maintenance laxatives at baseline (328/452), compared to 63.8% of subjects in the lower opioid dose group (i.e., 30-100 mg; 503/789). In the higher dose group 73.3% of the naldemedine arm and 71.8% of the placebo arm were taking maintenance laxatives, vs. 62.1% and 65.5% of the naldemedine and placebo arms of the lower dose group, respectively.

L876: ## ➤ **Other Concomitant Medications**
L877: ## *Non-opioid analgesic medications*
L878: In addition to opioids, 367 subjects (59%) in the naldemedine group and 403 subjects (65%) in the placebo group were receiving ≥1 non-opioid pain medication (Table 54). The most class of non-opioid analgesics in both groups were GABA analogues (&gt;1/3 of both treatment arms), such as gabapentin and pregabalin, and non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs). Use of NSAIDs, a class of medications with common adverse effect of constipation, was higher in the placebo group (36.0%, vs. 29.6% in the naldemedine arm).

L882: ## *Concomitant medications (excluding analgesics and laxatives)*
L883: Classes of medications used by ≥5% of the ITT population in Study V9235 are shown in Table 54. The most frequently used classes of concomitant medications (excluding analgesics and laxatives) in both groups were centrally acting muscle relaxants and benzodiazepines.
L884: The only classes of medications with &gt;4% difference in rate of use between treatment arms were SSRIs and centrally acting muscle relaxants, both of which are known to be associated with treatment-related constipation. In both cases, frequency of use was lower in the naldemedine arm, compared to placebo (17.2% and 35.4% in the naldemedine arm vs. 22.4% and 39.4% in the placebo arm, for SSRIs and centrally acting muscle relaxants, respectively).

L889: ## ➤ Medical history
L890: The sources of non-cancer pain reported by ≥1% of the ITT population in Study V9235 are shown in Table 56, and the most frequently reported medical history (reported by ≥10% of subjects) is shown in Table 57.
L891: Consistent with the study populations in Studies V9231 and V9232, back pain was, by far, the most common source of non-cancer pain in both groups, and was also the most common medical condition reported overall. The only clinically relevant differences between treatment arms are a higher rate of hypertension and dyslipidemia in the placebo arm [differences (naldemedine-placebo) of -2.7% and -4.4%, respectively]. The magnitude of these differences is relatively small; however, it is notable that these risk factors for MACE were more prevalent in the placebo arm of this long-term safety study. Both groups had similar percentages of subjects with diabetes mellitus.

L899: ## Efficacy Results – Primary Endpoint
L900: There was no primary efficacy endpoint in study V9235.
L901: ## Efficacy Results- Secondary and Exploratory Endpoints
L902: ## Laxative Use- Concomitant and Rescue Medications
L903: Of the 411 subjects in the naldemedine group on a laxative regimen at baseline, 314 subjects (76.4%) maintained a stable concomitant laxative regimen during study participation, representing 50.6% of the entire naldemedine treatment arm. In the placebo arm, 336/420 (80.0%) of the subjects on laxatives at baseline maintained their stable regimen for the duration of study participation (54.2% of the treatment arm).
L904: At the time of treatment completion or study termination, 40/411 (9.7%) subjects in the naldemedine arm and 25/420 (5.9%) subjects in the placebo arm with a baseline maintenance laxative regimen no longer had any ongoing laxative medications.
L905: Rescue laxative use during the study treatment period was reported in 70 subjects in the naldemedine arm (11.3%) vs. 79 subjects in the placebo arm (12.7%). In the naldemedine arm, rescue laxative use occurred in 53/411 of subjects (12.9%) on a stable laxative regimen at baseline vs. 17/210 of subjects (8.1%) without concomitant maintenance laxative use. In the placebo arm, the rate of rescue laxative use was more similar between subjects with and without baseline concomitant laxative regimens, i.e., 54/420 (12.9%) and 25/200 (12.5%), respectively.
L906: Fourteen subjects without laxative use at baseline (5 in the naldemedine arm and 9 in the placebo arm) had ≥1 ongoing concomitant laxative medication at the time of study completion/termination.

L910: ## Analysis Subgroups by Baseline Laxative Use
L911: As discussed previously, the analysis subgroups defined by the Applicant are based on a combination of baseline and outcome characteristics. In addition, inconsistencies were identified in the classification of study subjects based on laxative use at baseline and during study participation. The distribution of these subjects based on baseline laxative use is shown in Figure 20.

L913: ## Rescue Laxative Use
L914: The proportion of subjects in the ITT population with rescue laxative use during Study V9235 was 11.3% (70/621) in naldemedine arm, compared to 12.7% (79/620) in the placebo arm, and the difference between groups (1.4%) was not statistically significant.

L918: In both the stable laxative and no stable laxative subgroups, the proportion of subjects with rescue laxative use was higher in placebo arm, with p-values of 0.037 and 0.067, respectively. These data are misleading and provide an overestimate of the actual treatment effect on this outcome variable, and thus, should be used to conclude that naldemedine treatment is associated with a lower rescue laxative requirement, irrespective of concomitant maintenance laxative use. In the "other" subgroup, which consists of subjects with maintenance laxative usage that varied during study participation (i.e., increased, decreased, or discontinued all together), rescue laxative use was higher in the naldemedine arm.
L919: Results of these subgroup analyses, as well as those for other efficacy variables, need to be interpreted with caution because they are based on post-hoc definitions that are not defined according to baseline characteristics. The Applicant's method of subgroup division inappropriately excludes meaningful subjects from their baseline categories (e.g., subjects without maintenance laxative use at baseline that required initiation of a maintenance laxative regimen during study participation) and creates a new category without definable baseline or outcome characteristics. Therefore, results for individual subgroups are not interpretable and are only useful when assessed in the context of results for the overall population.
L920: When rescue laxative use is analyzed based on subjects' baseline laxative use, a treatment difference is only observed for the subjects' without established laxative regimens at baseline. Although these subgroup results are likely less biased by the post-hoc nature of the analyses
L921: than those conducted by the Applicant, the interpretability of these data are limited by the loose criteria used to define baseline laxative use and lack of standardized procedures for concomitant laxative use.
L922: ## Change in Weekly BM Frequency from Baseline
L923: At 4 selected time points during the Treatment Period (Weeks 12, 24, 36, and 52), change in BM frequency per week from baseline was assessed using data from the Bowel Habits paper diary completed for the week prior to the specified time point. Frequency of BMs per week was calculated as the weekly average of observations in 7 days prior to each visit. At the time of data cutoff for the Core Period, all ongoing subjects had completed &gt;24 weeks of treatment. Therefore, efficacy analyses for Weeks 12 and 24 include data for the full ITT population, while Week 36 and 52 efficacy analyses were performed using incomplete datasets, consisting of 37% fewer subjects for the Week 36 assessment and 61% fewer subjects for the Week 52 assessment, compared to the Week 24 assessment. The numbers of subjects in each treatment arm with available efficacy data at each time are shown in the figure.
L924: *Note: The Applicant did not assess SBM frequency during study treatment, only BM frequency. Therefore, these data do not account for rescue medication use.*

L930: Similar to the rescue laxative data, analyses of BM frequency based on the Applicant's laxative subgroup definitions are difficult to interpret. In addition, the interpretability of BM frequency assessments are also impacted by the amount of missing data at Weeks 36 and 52 due to the data cutoff of the Core Period Data Set. For these reasons, subgroup analyses of BM frequency based on concomitant laxative use have not been included in this review.
L931: Following the completion of Study V9235, the Applicant submitted updated datasets of BM frequency that included data accrued during the Supplemental Period. These include additional efficacy data for 232 subjects at Week 36 (total 757 subjects; vs. 525 subjects in Core Dataset analyses) and 373 subjects at Week 52 (total 702 subjects; vs. 329 subjects in Core Dataset analyses).
L932: Analyses of mean changes in weekly BM frequency for the full study population (Core and Supplemental data) are shown in Table 61. With the full datasets, the difference between group means was lower at Week 36 and slightly higher at Week 52, but p-values were lower with the larger study population. (Note: formal efficacy analyses of the updated datasets were not performed by the Applicant.)
L933: Similar to findings in the pivotal studies, a substantial placebo effect was also observed in Study V9235. Assessment of treatment effect was also more challenging in this study because of highly variable concurrent laxative use and incomplete rescue laxative data. Nevertheless, the observed treatment effect in the naldemedine arm (i.e., increase in BM frequency from baseline) was similar at all timepoints between Weeks 12 to 52 (Figure 22), providing evidence of sustained treatment response over time.

L955: ## 7.1.2. Primary Endpoints
L956: For both efficacy clinical trials, Study V9231 and Study V9232, the proportion of SBM responders was higher in the naldemedine arm compared to the placebo arm, with statistically significant differences between treatment arms achieved in both trials (p-values 0.0020 and &lt;0.0001, respectively). Of note, the absolute responder rates for individual treatment arms and the differences between groups (13.0% and 18.9%, respectively) are comparable to those observed in clinical trials for other PAMORAs.

L961: Primary analyses for the primary endpoint of Study V921 and Study V9232 used a pro-rated value for weekly SBM frequency that used a correction factor for the number of days with completed eDiary entries for the week, provided that eDiary data were available for ≥4 days of that week. This method for handling missing eDiary data is based on an assumption that the likelihood of an SBM event is the same on days with and without completed eDiary entries. However, the principle of response bias would suggest that subjects would be more likely to complete eDiary entries on days with events to report (e.g., bowel movement event, use of rescue medication), which could result in an inflated difference between treatment groups.
L962: Among the sensitivity analyses conducted for the primary endpoint to address potential effects of missing data on efficacy results was the “modified worst case”, which defined weekly SBM frequency as the observed number of SBMs in a week, rather than the pro-rated SBM frequency corrected for the number of days with completed eDiary entries, but still with minimum 4 days with eDiary data to be considered evaluable. This analysis method yielded similar results to the primary analysis (Table 24), indicating that missing eDiary entries had an insignificant impact on efficacy results.
L963: ## 7.1.3. Secondary and Other Endpoints
L964: In both trials, the results for the naldemedine group were statistically better than placebo for all 4 key secondary endpoints.
L965: ## *Pooled Secondary Endpoint Analyses*
L966: Pooled analyses of phase 3 clinical trial data (Study V9231 and V9232) for the 4 key secondary endpoints using the Applicant's pre-specified statistical methodology are summarized in Table 63. As discussed in the reviews of the individual clinical trials in Section 6, the Applicant's use of pro-rated BM event frequencies relies on mathematical correction for the number of days/week with completed diary entries, which is not considered appropriate by this reviewer due to the risk of introducing bias into the analyses in favor of the naldemedine treatment arm. This concern is supported by the comparison between analyses of adjusted and unadjusted data (Table 64), which shows the increase in frequency after adjustment was consistency disproportionately greater in the naldemedine group than the placebo group, suggesting a response bias in favor of the naldemedine group

L974: Although results of secondary endpoints achieved statistical significance, calculated differences between group means for the differences are not inherently clinical meaningful, and the minimum difference between group means for each of these secondary endpoints that would represent a clinically meaningful treatment effect is not known.
L975: In an effort to determine the clinical meaningfulness of secondary endpoint data, the Agency requested that the Applicant construct cumulative distribution function (CDF) plots of subject global satisfaction (SGS) scores and secondary endpoint data (figures located in Appendix, Section 13.4). These analyses demonstrated a positive correlation between increase in each of the BM events and higher SGS score, suggesting clinically meaningful treatment benefit. However, the SGS questionnaire was not considered adequately worded to elicit responses regarding satisfaction specifically related to symptoms of constipation.
L976: ## 7.1.1. Dose and Dose-Response
L977: Naldemedine 0.2 mg once daily was the only dose evaluated in the naldemedine phase 3 clinical trial program.
L978: ## 7.1.2. Dose/Dose Response
L979: Only 1 naldemedine dosage (0.2 mg once daily) was evaluated in controlled phase 3 clinical trials, including Study V9231. Therefore, analyses of dose-response are limited to the phase 2 clinical trial (see Section 5.5.2).
L980: ## Onset, Duration, and Durability of Efficacy Effects
L981: The responder definition for primary efficacy analyses includes a specific criterion (i.e., weekly response for 3 of the last 4 weeks of the treatment period) to incorporate a requirement for durability of response. In addition, onset and durability of efficacy is shown by analysis of weekly SBM over time, which demonstrates response starting during Week 1, which was maintained throughout the study treatment period. (Figure 24) Similar maintenance of response was observed for other evaluated BM events. Figure 25 shows the frequency of SBM/week with BSS score of 3 or 4 (desirable stool consistency).

L987: ## 7.1.3. Subpopulations
L988: Subpopulation analyses were conducted for the primary endpoint of the 2 main efficacy trials (Study V9231 and V9232). Overall, findings were consistent among different subgroups in both studies, demonstrating higher response rate in subjects treated with naldemedine (vs. placebo). The only exception was the black racial subgroup in study V9232. However, the racial distribution of all studies was disproportionately white (approximately 80% of the pooled ITT population), and the black racial subgroup comprised only 18.5% and 15.5% of the ITT population of Study V9231 and V9232, respectively.
L989: The only notable demographic subgroup analysis was age, which showed higher response rate among naldemedine-treated subjects aged &lt;65, compared to naldemedine-treated subjects ≥65 years old. However, treatment effect (i.e., difference in proportion of responders between the naldemedine and placebo arms) was similar between age groups. The lower response rate in subjects aged ≥65 years old, regardless of treatment arm may be attributable to effects of age-related idiopathic constipation.

L994: In addition, the Applicant conducted subgroup analyses based on opioid use. These results (Figure 26) demonstrate similar results for the 2 opioid dose strata. When analyzed by narrower ranges of opioid doses, results were generally similar across groups and comparable to pooled results, with the exception of subjects with baseline TDD of &gt;400 mg morphine equivalents (the highest strata), which had a difference in response rate &gt;2-fold higher than the pooled population and ~2-fold higher than the other subgroups. L996: Compared to opioid dose-based groups, greater variability in comparative response rates was observed among opioid medication types. However, these subsets were also generally smaller than the opioid dose groups. For the different opioid subtypes, the most notable differences were observed for the morphine and hydromorphone subgroups, who had treatment differences in response rate favoring placebo (\Delta -5.7% and -1.6%, respectively), and the methadone subgroup, who had the largest observable treatment difference (\Delta 45.8%). For the morphine and methadone subgroups, the 95% confidence intervals for the difference in proportions excluded the value for the pooled population (16%).

L1000: ## 8 Review of Safety
L1001: ## Safety Review Approach
L1002: The safety analysis dataset for this application consists of a pooled placebo-controlled safety population of randomized subjects who received at least 1 dose of study drug in the naldemedine clinical trial, focusing primarily on the data analyses conducted in the target (proposed) treatment population—i.e., patients with OIC and chronic, non-cancer pain—for which the majority of the safety data were obtained from 3 double-blind, placebo-controlled phase 3 trials, Studies V9231, V9232, and V9235. However, Study V9235 is the only one of these 3 trials with safety data in patients receiving extended study treatment (naldemedine or
L1003: placebo).
L1004: While Studies V9231 and V9232 were replicate efficacy trials in which subjects received up to 12 weeks of study treatment, Study V9235 was a 52-week trial whose primary objective was to evaluate the long-term safety of naldemedine in patients with OIC and chronic, non-cancer pain. (Refer to Section 5 for discussion of each of the trial's design and conduct.) Safety data were analyzed for each individual trial's safety population. Because study procedures were the same for Studies V9231 and V9232, analyses of their pooled safety analysis populations were also performed, permitting a broader safety evaluation across the target treatment population. Safety data from other clinical trials (phase 1 trials in healthy individuals, phase 2 trials in patients with OIC and chronic, non-cancer pain, and all trials in patients with OIC and cancer-related pain) are considered supplementary, and in general, only notable findings are reported.
L1005: This review includes focused analyses performed to assess for 2 specific potential safety concerns—opiate withdrawal and major adverse cardiac events (MACE), both of which were identified based on findings from previous clinical trials of other PAMORAs.
L1006: Opioid withdrawal symptoms, which suggest the mu opioid antagonist effects in OIC may not be limited to peripheral receptors in all patients, have been noted in the review of safety data from OIC clinical trials. Therefore, in addition to assessment of AEs for evidence of opioid withdrawal, standard evaluation includes Opioid Withdrawal Scales, conducted at a frequency to provide clinically meaningful information. Also, because opioid withdrawal represents a constellation of relatively non-specific symptom, pre-defined adverse events potentially related to opioid withdrawal are needed, as well as use of a standardized MedRA Query (SMQ) of Drug Withdrawal Syndrome.
L1007: Unlike the risk of opiate withdrawal, the clinical significance of MACE and their relationship to study treatment observed in clinical trials of another PAMORA, Enetereg, have not been determined. The biological plausibility of these findings is substantial enough to support the safety concerns raised during this trial. However, it remains unclear whether this should be deemed a class-wide concern, or whether these data represent a true safety signal.
L1008: ## 8.2. Review of the Safety Database
L1009: ## 8.2.1. Overall Exposure
L1010: A total of 2136 subjects were exposed to naldemedine (\ge 1 dose) throughout the entire naldemedine clinical development program, of which 1163 were patients with OIC and chronic, non-cancer pain enrolled in Phase 3 placebo-controlled clinical trials.
L1011: ## 8.2.2. Adequacy of the safety database:
L1012: As noted by the Applicant, the size of the naldemedine safety database and extent of exposure in the is similar to clinical development programs for other PAMORAs and follows the ICH guideline's general recommendation for long-term treatment of non-life-threatening conditions (i.e., 1500 subjects exposed in multiple dose studies, with 300-600 exposed for 6 months and 100 exposed for 1 year, at relevant doses). However, it is important to note that rare safety events often go undetected prior to approval because exposure to a drug during clinical trials is relatively small compared to the number of patients treated after approval. Therefore, larger safety databases would be required to quantify the rate of specific low-frequency AE prior to marketing approval.
L1013: Due to unresolved cardiovascular safety concerns identified based on Entereg clinical trial data, an advisory committee meeting (AADPAC) was held to in June 2014 to discuss whether pre-approval, prospective studies are needed to assess for risk of MACE for any or all of the PAMORAs being developed for treatment of OIC in non-cancer pain patients.
L1014: ## SUMMARY OF ADVISORY COMMITTEE RECOMMENDATIONS:
L1015: The gold standard for such assessments is a CVOT are resource intensive and may be impractical for OIC drug programs. Their feasibility is limited by the large sample size and long duration of exposure needed to observe a sufficient number of events to attain a desired level of statistical power, and draw meaningful conclusions on causality.
L1016: Alternative options to obtaining CV data using a combination of pre-approval and post-marketing observational data were discussed; taking into consideration causality is extremely difficult to establish without a concurrent control group, unless the events would be exceedingly rare in the patient population.
L1017: Following the AC meeting, the Applicant stated an intention to conduct a randomized (1:1), placebo-controlled safety trial in 1500-patients randomized 1:1 to receive naldemedine or placebo. Although the study was intended as a CVOT, no specific enrichment strategies were discussed.
L1018: During the EOP2 meeting, the sponsor proposed to change the primary endpoint of Study V9235 to the general safety of naldemedine and decrease the planned population to 1200 subjects. Because no formal calculations were used to determine sample size for this trial, this study was not designed to rule out a prespecified increase in CV risk with a cumulative exposure insufficient to observe a sufficient number of events to attain a desired level of statistical power (given background event rate).
L1019: By contrast, sample sizes for event-driven trials such as CVOTs are powered around the number of events and the amount of patient-years of exposure needed to observe these events. This is calculated based on a pre-specified risk margin (e.g. hazard ratio risk margin), an assumed true
L1020: risk of the treatment related to the control, and the desired level of power (typically 90% for CVOTs).
L1021: Given the challenges of conducting formal CVOTs, the Division agreed to the proposed changes in the Study V9235 trial design. However, the Division cautioned the Applicant that drop-out rates in long-term trials for OIC have historically been ~30%, notably higher than the Applicant's predicted rate of 10%, and safety events might not be attributable to the assigned treatment if a large number of subjects discontinue treatment early in the study.
L1022: Although Study V9235 was not conducted as a formal CVOT, this study included pre-specified definitions of MACE, procedures to collect information relevant to the assessment of MACE events, and prospective, independent, external adjudication for all potential major adverse CV events (MACE), all in accordance with current Division recommendation for developing mu opioid receptor antagonists for OIC. Therefore, Study V9235 is the first long-term safety trial of a PAMORA that incorporated vigorous, comprehensive procedures for prospective and objective assessments for MACE and other class-wide safety concerns. However, it is important to note that even with a pre-specified plan for ascertainment of CV events, given the typically limited sample size and duration of the controlled studies, it may be difficult to make valid conclusions regarding the significance of rare events, even if numerical imbalances among treatment arms are observed.
L1023: Because pre-marketing trials are without prespecified features of a CVOT and are of a size that could detect only large increases in MACE risk, and then only if patients with higher cardiovascular risk were included, post-marketing observational studies would be needed to further quantify the potential risk of MACE associated with use of these drugs in real-world healthcare settings and with large numbers of patients, as communicated by most of the AADPAC members.
L1024: ## 8.3. Adequacy of Applicant's Clinical Safety Assessments
L1025: ## 8.3.1. Issues Regarding Data Integrity and Submission Quality
L1026: The quality of this submission was good, and the submission was well organized. The information in the submission provided all elements as agreed upon during the pre-NDA meeting, with the minor exception of the protocol deviations for Study V9235 and the need for minor data clarifications.
L1027: ## 8.3.2. Categorization of Adverse Events
L1028: ## General
L1029: Adverse events were coded with MedDRA (Version 16.0) terms and then summarized by SOC and preferred term.
L1030: Treatment emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were defined as those that occurred after the initial dose of study drug up to 28 days after the final dose of study drug.
L1031: Causality: the relationship of the adverse event to the study drug was to be determined by the Investigator according to the following criteria
L1032: - Not related: The event could be clearly explained by a causative factor other than the study drug, or there was no reasonable temporal relationship between administration of the study drug and the occurrence of the adverse event
- Possibly related: Involvement of other causative factors than the study drug could be considered, but a causal relationship with the study drug could not be ruled out
- Probably related: Involvement of other causative factors than the study drug was unlikely
- Definitely related: There was a reasonable temporal relationship between administration of the study drug and occurrence of the adverse event, and there were no other causative factors other than the study drug that could explain the event
L1033: The severity of the adverse event was to be assessed by the Investigator according to the following definitions:
L1034: - Mild: The subject experienced awareness of symptoms but these were easily tolerated or managed without specific treatment
- Moderate: The subject experienced discomfort enough to cause interference with usual activity, and/or the condition required specific treatment
- Severe: The subject was incapacitated with inability to work or do usual activity, and/or the event required significant treatment measures
L1035: ## Abuse, Misuse, Overdose, and Medication Error
L1036: Immediate reporting using a Special Situation Report Form was required for identified cases of abuse, misuse, overdose, and medication error, defined as the following:
L1037: - Abuse: persistent or sporadic, intentional excessive use of an investigational product, which was accompanied by harmful physical or psychological effect
- Misuse: intentional and inappropriate use of an investigational product(s) other than as directed or indicated at any dose. Cases of subjects missing doses of investigational product were not considered reportable as misuse
- Overdose – Intentional or unintentional intake of a dose of investigational product(s) higher than the assigned dose in the protocol
L1038: - Medication Error – Any unintended error in the prescribing, dispensing, or administration of an investigational product.
L1039: ## **Adjudication of Major Cardiovascular Events**
L1040: In Studies V9231, V9232 and V9235, blinded medical review and adjudication of all events known or suspected to be a MACE was performed by an external independent Cardiovascular Adjudication Committee (CAC) consisting of experienced cardiologists. The following procedures were implemented to optimize identification and review of all cardiovascular events:
L1041: - All treatment-emergent adverse events related to MACE were adjudicated by the CAC and were summarized by the following MACE type and treatment group: CV death, myocardial infarction, and cerebrovascular accident (stroke).
- Adjudication was also performed for cases of chest pain or other symptoms typical of acute coronary syndrome, including symptoms that may have overlapped with opioid withdrawal (ie, COWS, SOWS) with detailed documentation of the potential presence of opioid withdrawal symptoms (ie, COWS, SOWS) for each case
- Investigators were responsible for assessment of all CV-related events, with particular attention to MACE including CV death, myocardial infarction, and cerebrovascular accident.
- The Applicant specified a reporting requirement for any suspected CV events via electronic data capture (EDC) within 24 hours of awareness to ensure timely CAC adjudication. Investigators were required to collect documents related to the event (eg, ECGs, laboratory data, adverse event, SAE information, etc.) and assemble an event-specific package that was submitted to the CAC for review and adjudication. A checklist of expected documents for the type of event was provided to Investigators following EDC notification to assist with package completion.
- The Applicant and medical monitor conducted regular reviews of adverse events and SAEs to identify any additional CV-related safety events that may not have been reported by Investigators. For additional CV-related events identified as a result of the Applicant's review, Investigators were asked to provide detailed information for each event. The trial database was also periodically searched using Standard MedDRA Queries (Cardiac Arrhythmias SMQ, Cardiac Failure SMQ, Cardiomyopathy SMQ, Cerebrovascular disorders SMQ, Embolic and Thrombotic Events SMQ, Ischemic heart disease SMQ, Shock SMQ, and Torsade de pointes/QT prolongation SMQ) and any newly identified events were considered for adjudication.
L1042: ## 8.3.3. Routine Clinical Tests
L1043: ## **Standard Safety Assessments**
L1044: Blood samples for chemistry and hematology and urine samples for urinalysis were to be collected at times indicated in Section 6.3.1. In addition, a separate Liver Event Form was provided for events which met the criteria prespecified in the protocol.
L1045: ## **Other Safety Assessments: Tools Specific to this Application/Class of medications**
L1046: ## Questionnaire Assessments for Opiate Withdrawal Signs and Symptoms
L1047: - Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale (COWS):Completed by site personnel at study site
- 11-item questionnaire
- Total scores used to assess overall severity of withdrawal(mild= 5 to 12; moderate= 13 to 24; moderately severe= 25 to 36; severe &gt;36)
- V9231 and V9232The COWS assessment was to be performed at:Visit 2 (Day 1) (at pre-dose and at 60, 90, 120 minutes post dose [\pm10 minutes])
- Visit 3 (Week 1), Visit 4 (Week 2), Visit 5 (Week 4), Visit 6 (Week 8), and Visit 7 (Week 12) (or at Early Termination), at any time during the visit
- If a COWS total score was \ge5 or indicated a worsening of withdrawal symptoms, the Investigator or designee was to complete a supplemental Elevated COWS Evaluation Form
- Data analysis:Summary statistics of the total COWS score (sum of scores on all items) and change from baseline to each scheduled visit were calculated by treatment group.
- Plots of means of the absolute value (and their standard deviation) over time were presented by treatment group.
- Analysis of COWS score were performed excluding abdominal pain
- Subjective Opiate Withdrawal Scale (SOWS):Completed by16-item questionnaire, 5-point rating scale (0=not at all; 1=a little; 2=moderate; 3=quite a bit; and 4=extreme)
- Used to identify subjects with possible opioid withdrawalScores do not correlate with specific levels of withdrawal
- Useful to identify and track potential opioid withdrawal symptoms
L1048: - If a SOWS assessment indicated worsening of withdrawal symptoms, the Investigator or designee completed a supplemental Elevated COWS Evaluation Form to further assess for opioid withdrawal and an unscheduled follow-up COWS assessment was administered as deemed necessary by the Investigator
- The SOWS assessment was to be performed at:Visit 2 (Day 1) (at pre-dose and at 1 hour post dose [\pm10 minutes])
- Between Visit 2 (Day 1) and Visit 4 (Week 2) (ie, first 14 days on study drug) SOWS was to be performed daily by the subject. Subjects performed the SOWS approximately 1 hour after dosing study drug
- Visit 4 (Week 2), Visit 5 (Week 4), Visit 6 (Week 8), and Visit 7 (Week 12) or Early Termination, and at any time during the visit
- Means of the total SOWS score were calculated for each week using the formula: (Total of week's SOWS scores) / (Number of days of observation for the week)Summary statistics of the total SOWS score and change from baseline to each scheduled visit were calculated by treatment group.
- If multiple records were available in a day, the highest total score was used
- Analysis of SOWS score with and without abdominal pain questions
L1049: ## Pain Assessments
L1050: - 11-point Numeric Rating Scale (NRS): measurement of severity of painCompleted by
- Rating scale: 0 to 10 (0=no pain and 10=worst possible pain(0, no pain; 1 to 3, mild pain; 4 to 6, moderate pain; and 7 to 10, severe pain)
- Studies V9231 and V9232: Completed at the study site at every visit Visit 2 (Day 1) and through Visit 7 (Week 12) or Early Termination
- Data analyses:Summary statistics of the NRS score for pain evaluation and change from baseline to each scheduled visit were calculated by treatment group.
- Plots of means (standard deviation) over time by treatment group
- Summary of outliers (number and percentage) by treatment group
- Opioid Analgesics: assessment of pain based on change in average total daily and weekly dose of opioids from baseline

L1059: ## 8.4. Safety Results
L1060: ## Deaths
L1061: Nine deaths occurred during the naldemedine clinical trials relevant to the proposed indication—5 in the naldemedine group and 4 in the placebo group (summarized in Table 65). Eight of the 9 deaths (4 in each group) occurred during Study V9235, the 52-week safety trial, CDER Clinical Review Template 2015 Edition

*Version date: June 25, 2015 for initial rollout (NME/original BLA reviews)*
L1062: and 1 death (in the naldemedine group) occurred during the 12-week trials. No deaths occurred in Phase 1 or Phase 2 trials.
L1063: All deaths, except Subject 52415-004, who died 97 days after study discontinuation due to fatal TEAE of adenocarcinoma, occurred while subjects were active clinical trial participants.
L1064: In 4 cases, the fatal SAE was not considered by the Applicant to be treatment-emergent because the calculated AE onset was &gt;14 days after “last known dose” of study drug. However, these calculations were based on imputed data using flawed methodology. In all 4 cases, medication bottles were not returned to the study site for accountability. Therefore, the Applicant imputed the date of last dose of study drug as the last dose prior to the subject’s last study visit (last accounting of drug doses). However, the Applicant confirmed that all 4 of these subjects were actively participating in the trial at the time of AE onset and received a per-protocol supply of medication at their last study visit. Only 1 of the 4 subjects missed the last study visit prior to AE onset—Subject 52352-002 (cardiopulmonary arrest presumed to be caused by accidental overdose) who missed the last scheduled study visit 7 days prior to AE onset. Assuming regular treatment compliance, all 4 of these fatal AEs were treatment-emergent.
L1065: None of the 9 deaths were considered by the Investigator to be related to study treatment. Four events (2 in each treatment arm) were adjudicated by the CAC as cardiovascular deaths.
L1066: Six deaths (3 in each treatment arm) were unwitnessed cardiac/cardiopulmonary arrests, of which 3 deaths (1 in the naldemedine arm and 2 in the placebo arm) were adjudicated by the CAC as MACE. No post-mortem evaluation was performed in any of these 6 cases. In general, minimal clinical information was available, and all assigned causes of death for these subjects were presumptive, without any supportive data.
L1067: As part of the safety evaluation, data from other naldemedine OIC clinical trials, including phase 3 trials for patients with cancer-related pain and the long-term open-label phase 3 clinical trial (Study 1336V9238) in adults with non-cancer pain (Japan only), were assessed for evidence of previously unrecognized safety signals. In studies of subjects with cancer-related pain, deaths were generally related to subjects’ underlying malignancy, without evidence of treatment effect. In Study 1336V9238 (V9238), one death (unwitnessed event of unknown cause) occurred in a 51-year-old male after 308 days of naldemedine exposure. A more detailed description of this event is included in the narratives below.
L1068: Abbreviations used in table: COPD, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease; CVA, cerebrovascular accident; UNK, unknown
* The last day of study drug administration unknown because study medication bottles were not returned to the clinical site for accountability. The
investigator imputed the date of last study drug administration as the last date confirmed by the clinical trial site—the last dose prior to the subject's
last visit. These subjects were active clinical trial participants at the time of death. A new supply of study medication would have been dispensed at
the subject's last visit. Assuming regular treatment compliance, these events are all treatment-emergent (&lt;14 days after last dose). Subject 52352-002
would likely have been off-treatment due to missed visit 7 days prior to death.

L1073: ## NARRATIVES OF DEATHS
L1074: ## Naldemedine (n=5)
L1075: - Subject 22359-018, a 37-year-old female was reported to a sudden death due to unwitnessed cardiopulmonary arrest on Day 9. The subject was found unresponsive after an unwitnessed event. The subject was apneic and pulseless when emergency medical services arrived. Cardiopulmonary resuscitation was initiated, including administration of naloxone. In the emergency department, global hypoxic brain injury was diagnosed by CT scan, and medical care was withdrawn. The cause of death was listed as drug overdose and cardiopulmonary arrest. No diagnostic or clinical information was available to support this diagnosis. The only toxicology studies obtained were an acetaminophen and ethanol level. No other drug levels were obtained. No autopsy was done.
L1076: Prior medical history included obesity (BMI = 32.1 \text{ kg/m}^2), osteoarthritis, degenerative disc disease, insomnia, peripheral neuropathy, depression, anxiety, bipolar disorder, attention deficit hyperactivity disorder, hypertension, and headaches. The subject also had a history of suicide attempts, prior history of methadone overdose, and social history notable for current tobacco use and occasional marijuana use.
L1077: Reviewer comment: The cause of this unwitnessed event of cardiopulmonary arrest resulting in death is unknown. No toxicology studies or post-mortem evaluation were performed, and the available data do not provide diagnostic information. Therefore, a cardiovascular event cannot be excluded. This case is included in assessments of MACE risk, as well as risk of overdose.
L1078: - Subject 52387-009, a 58-year-old female with fatal event of COPD exacerbation (onset Day 155). The subject died during the hospital admission on Study Day 162 (hospital day 8). The cause of death was acute exacerbation of COPD with tobacco use reported as a contributing factor.
L1079: Prior medical history included COPD, obesity (BMI=37.3 \text{ kg/m}^2), cardiac catheterization, hypothyroidism, fibromyalgia, intervertebral disc herniation, depression, anxiety, menopause, and restless leg syndrome. No autopsy was done.
L1080: Earlier in the study (Day 87), the subject also had a non-serious, TEAE of peripheral edema that was moderate in severity. No action was taken with the study drug due to the AE of peripheral edema. The edema was reported as resolving at the time of last follow-up. The contact between site personnel and the subject was by phone on Day 126, at which time the subject reported no changes in health or medication.
L1081: The last dose of study drug was reported as Day 99 (imputed date based on the subject's last study visit). The subject was actively participating in the clinical trial at the time of SAE onset. No information was provided regarding study treatment during the subject's hospitalization.
L1082: *Reviewer comment: This death was caused by an exacerbation of a pre-existing condition. It cannot be determined whether study treatment was a contributing factor in the severity or outcome of this event.*
L1083: - Subject 52415-004, a 52-year-old, White female with fatal SAE of Stage IV adenocarcinoma of the lung (onset Day 72). The subject presented to the emergency room on Study Day 72 with chest pain and dyspnea and was subsequently hospitalized. Of note, the patient had also presented with dyspnea on Study Day 63, at which time only showed cardiomegaly (no pneumonia). Repeat chest x-ray on Day 72 showed prominent vasculature. The subject was hospitalized from Day 72 to day 78, during which time the subject was diagnosed with multiple SAEs, including the fatal SAE of Stage IV adenocarcinoma.Deep venous thrombosis (popliteal vein) was diagnosed by Doppler ultrasound and confirmed by venogram
- Pulmonary embolism- venocaval filter placed based on venacavogram
- Myocardial infarction with abnormal ECG and findings of arterial blockage and ischemia on cardiac catheterization
- Adenocarcinoma (fatal SAE) was diagnosed initially based on a bloody pericardial fluid sample obtained on Day 76 which demonstrated malignant cells, consistent with adenocarcinoma. Based on additional evaluation determined to be metastatic adenocarcinoma with primary pulmonary malignancy.
L1084: Prior medical history included obesity (BMI=35.8 kg/m2), anxiety, B12 deficiency, cervicalgia/chronic neck pain, chronic bronchitis (COPD), intervertebral disc herniation and degeneration, depression, bilateral deep vein thrombosis of the lower extremities, edema, fibromyalgia, hysterectomy, migraine headache, muscle spasms, neuropathic pain, osteoarthritis, and pneumothorax syndrome.
L1085: Study drug was discontinued during hospitalization on Day 72 (admission to the hospital). The investigator considered the events of pulmonary embolism and myocardial infarction as severe in severity and possibly related to study drug. The investigator considered the event of DVT as moderate in severity and possibly related to study drug. The investigator considered the event of Stage 4 adenocarcinoma as severe in severity and not related to study drug.
L1086: *Reviewer comment: This subject had multiple diagnoses made during the hospitalization for the fatal SAE. While this reviewer agrees that the fatal SAE of adenocarcinoma is unrelated to study treatment. The other SAEs of deep venous thrombosis, pulmonary embolism, and myocardial infarction represent events of interest and are discussed elsewhere.*
L1087: - Subject 52375-007, a 53-year-old female, was found dead at home on Study Day 350. An autopsy was not performed. The cause of the death as captured in the death certificate was suspected cerebrovascular accident. The event was adjudicated by the CAC as an
L1088: undetermined cause of death.
L1089: Prior medical history included chronic headaches (hemicranias continua), abnormal gait with frequent falls, orthostatic hypotension, restless leg syndrome, bronchitis, sinus infection, septal perforation, knee pain, depression, anxiety, asthma, and postmenopause.
L1090: The subject was actively participating in the clinical trial at the time of death.
L1091: *Reviewer comment: The case is one of the unwitnessed fatal events for which the cause of cardiopulmonary arrest is unknown. No toxicology studies or post-mortem evaluation were performed, and the available data do not provide diagnostic information.*
L1092: - Subject 52449-005, a 49-year-old male, was found dead in the bathroom on Day 350, presumed to have died 4 days earlier (Day 346). An autopsy was not performed. The cause of death was captured in the death certificate as myocardial infarction due to or as a consequence of diabetes. This event was adjudicated by the CAC as a cardiovascular death.
L1093: Medical history included diabetes mellitus type 1, acute myocardial infarction, hypercholesterolemia, hypertension, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, spinal osteoarthritis, cervical radiculitis, cervical spondylosis, chronic back pain, chronic neck pain, rotator cuff syndrome, headaches, anxiety, depression, and overweight (BMI = 27.7 \text{ kg/m}^2).
L1094: The subject was actively participating in the clinical trial at the time of death.
L1095: *Reviewer comment: The case is one of the unwitnessed fatal events for which the cause of cardiopulmonary arrest is unknown. No diagnostic studies or post-mortem evaluation were performed, and the available data do not provide diagnostic information.*
L1096: ## Placebo (n=4)
L1097: - Subject 52352-002, a 52-year-old male, with death due to cardiopulmonary arrest. The subject was found dead by in his bed. Cause of death was listed as accidental opioid overdose. No laboratory evaluation was performed. No autopsy was done.
L1098: The subject had a previous medical history of BMI in the overweight range (BMI=28.1 \text{ kg/m}^2), hypercholesterolemia, type 2 diabetes, lower back pain/ injuries, and peripheral neuropathy.
L1099: The subject missed Visit 5 (scheduled on Day 43). The site attempted unsuccessfully to contact the subject on the day of the missed visit and was later informed of the subject's death by the subject's son. The cause of death of "overdose" was presumptive based on the subject's history of chronic opioid use.
L1100: *Reviewer comment: The case is one of the unwitnessed fatal events for which the cause of cardiopulmonary arrest is unknown. No toxicology studies or post-mortem evaluation were performed, and the available data do not provide diagnostic information. A cardiovascular event cannot be excluded.*
L1101: - Subject 52437-005, a 64-year-old male found on Day 28. The subject was found on the bathroom floor by his wife. Upon arrival of EMS, the subject was pulseless and apneic. Cardiopulmonary resuscitation was initiated, including intubation, chest compressions, and administration of naloxone and epinephrine. The electrocardiogram reportedly showed asystole throughout the process, except for a brief period of pulseless electrical activity. Based on advanced directives specifying that the subject did not want intubation, excessive resuscitation, or transportation to a facility, EMS obtained permission to cease resuscitative events. The presumed etiology was cardiac. The cause of death was listed as cardiac arrest and atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease. The event was adjudicated by the CAC as a cardiovascular death.
L1102: Prior medical history was notable for atrial fibrillation, COPD, degenerative intervertebral disc disease, depression, hypercholesterolemia, hypertension, osteoarthritis, lower extremity edema, heavy tobacco use (40 cigarettes per day since 1960), and BMI in the overweight range (\text{BMI}=28.8 \text{ kg/m}^2).
L1103: *Reviewer comment: The case is one of the unwitnessed fatal events for which the cause of cardiopulmonary arrest is unknown. No diagnostic studies or post-mortem evaluation were performed, and the available data do not provide diagnostic information.*
L1104: - Subject 52442-017, a 49-year-old White female with an SAE found dead at home by her husband on Day 28. Emergency services were contacted but the subject was assessed to be deceased and no resuscitation was performed. No autopsy or examination was done. The cause of death was listed as complication of atherosclerotic heart disease (Applicant AE term "arteriosclerosis"). The event was adjudicated by the CAC as a cardiovascular death.
L1105: Prior medical history included obesity (\text{BMI}=37.1 \text{ kg/m}^2), diabetes mellitus type 2 with diabetic neuropathy, COPD, asthma, anxiety, anemia, GERD, lumbar intervertebral disc degeneration, and hemorrhoids.
L1106: *Reviewer comment: The case is one of the unwitnessed fatal events for which the cause of cardiopulmonary arrest is unknown. No diagnostic studies or post-mortem evaluation were performed, and the available data do not provide diagnostic information.*
L1107: - Subject 52446-013, a 78-year-old female, experienced a fatal cerebrovascular accident (CVA) on Study Day 175. The subject was found unresponsive in the bathroom by her husband. Head CT showed likely basilar thrombosis. The initial treating hospital administered aspirin and ceftriaxone sodium. Based on the NIH stroke scoring system, TPA was not given. The subject was transferred another facility where a CT perfusion was performed, demonstrating abnormality in the cerebral blood flow in the brain stem. CT angiogram showed occlusion of the basilar artery with intraluminal thrombus in the distal basilar artery and perfusion abnormalities involving the midbrain and infero-posteromedial thalami bilaterally. Several attempts were made to perform a mechanical thrombectomy procedure, but were unsuccessful due to inability to catheterize either vertebral artery or
L1108: the right subclavian artery. The family requested initiation of comfort care, and the subject expired on Day 179. The event was adjudicated by the CAC as a cardiovascular death.
L1109: Medical history included obesity (BMI=38.1 kg/m2), hypercholesterolemia, hypertension asthma, osteoarthritis, bilateral knee replacements, multiple fractures, peripheral neuropathy, GERD, hiatal hernia (post repair), and tremor.
L1110: The subject was actively participating in the clinical trial at the time of AE onset. The drug was withdrawn following hospitalization.
L1111: Reviewer comment: This is a confirmed case of MACE in a subject receiving placebo.
L1112: **Additional Narrative:** Study V9238 (open-label study in Japanese subjects only)
L1113: - Subject ID: 2NW002- 51-year-old male (50 years old at study enrollment) with with unwitnessed death of unknown cause on Study Day 309. Prior medical history was most notable for pulmonary embolism (PE) and deep vein thrombosis (DVT), for which the subject was on anticoagulation. Other medical history included spinal osteoarthritis, muscle spasms, insomnia, and tinnitus. BMI was 25.2 kg/m2 (overweight). Concomitant medications included warfarin (see below), celecoxib, eperisone (muscle antispasmodic), etizolam, somazolam, sennoside laxative, neurotropin, diclofenac topical gel, ramelteon, amlodipine, mepivacaine injection, magnesium oxide, and acetaminophen. Opioid medications included morphine, tramadol, and transdermal fentanyl.
L1114: At the time of enrollment, the subject was on warfarin treatment for prophylaxis of thrombosis due to prior history of PE and DVT. Prior to the event, in Month 7 of study treatment, warfarin treatment was interrupted for 1 week due to prolonged prothrombin time (PT), then 3 weeks later, the subject's anticoagulant medication was switched from warfarin to edoxaban tosilate hydrate. Two months later (Study Day 308), the subject was found dead at home. An autopsy could not reveal the cause of death.
L1115: During study participation, the subject also experienced non-serious AEs of diarrhea (onset Day 2), vomiting (onset Day 114), contusion (onset Day 223), and muscle spasms (onset Day 225).
L1116: Reviewer Comment: Study V9238 is an open-label, single-arm study. Therefore, there is no comparator to detect increased risk of treatment over background event rate. This fatal event represents a case of MACE and another sudden, unwitnessed cardiac/cardio pulmonary arrest of unknown cause. This subject did not have reported history of significant cardiac risk factors, although concomitant medications include amlodipine, which suggests possible history of hypertension. The autopsy was uninformative and did not reveal evidence of recurrence of PE or other thrombotic event.
L1117: ## 8.4.2. Serious Adverse Events
L1118: A total of 174 subjects in Phase 3 placebo-controlled safety population had ≥1 treatment-emergent serious adverse event (SAE)—83 in the naldemedine arm (7.1%) vs. 91 in the placebo arm (7.8%). A total of 143 SAEs were reported, of which 18 were identified by this reviewer as definitely having an AE onset &gt;5 days after discontinuation of treatment (i.e., not “on-treatment”). (Table 68)
L1119: Not surprisingly, the total number of SAEs and percentage of subjects with ≥1 SAE were 3 to 4-fold higher in Study V9235 than Studies V9231 and V9232.
L1120: All on-treatment SAEs (i.e., during the period from the first dose of study drug to 5 days after drug discontinuation) are shown in Table 67 and Table 68 [SAEs that occurred in ≥1 subjects are summarized in Table 67, and SAEs occurring in only a single individual are listed in Table 68.] In both groups, the most common SAEs were pneumonia and arthritis (the majority of arthritis SAEs were classified as serious because subjects were hospitalized for surgical procedures).

L1133: (Source: Reviewer's table)
L1134: Gastrointestinal SAEs were reported in 23 subjects with in the pooled phase 3 placebo-controlled population, with similar numbers of events in both treatment arms (12 in the naldemedine group and 11 in the placebo arm). Narratives for these events are provided in Table 53.
L1135: In general, the types of GI SAEs reported were representative of the overall GI-related TEAE data (see Sections 8.4.4 and 8.4.5), in which early naldemedine-GI toxicity was noted, and other types of GI events were also commonly due to subject's opioid medications and other underlying conditions. Notably, the majority of patients in both groups with GI-related SAEs were female (10/12 in the naldemedine arm and 10/11 in the placebo arm).
L1136: Pain-related SAEs comprised the largest group of SAEs in Study V9235; however, only 4 on-treatment pain-related SAEs were identified that occurred during Studies V9231 and V9232—2 in the naldemedine arm (Subject 12205-002 with worsening back pain and Subject 22326-007 with sciatica) and 2 in the placebo arm (Subject 12211-003 with osteoarthritis aggravation and Subject 22302-003 with musculoskeletal chest pain).
L1137: By contrast, pain-related SAEs were reported in 36 subjects in Study V9235 (2.9%)— 17 subjects in the naldemedine group and 19 subjects in the placebo group. For 20 of these subjects (10 in each group), these events represented worsening of their underlying joint disease, resulting in surgery. In the naldemedine group, 7 of the 10 surgeries were performed after Study Day 120 (the other 3 were performed on days 37, 49, and 63), while the earliest surgery in the placebo group was performed on Day 120. The higher incidence of pain-related SAEs in the 52-week, compared to the 12-week studies, would be expected due to progression of subjects' underlying condition over time. Review of these data did not identify any unexpected differences between treatment arms, with the exception of appendicitis, which was reported only in 2 naldemedine-treated subjects (1 in the 12-week studies and 1 in the 52-week study). The number of SAEs of appendicitis is too low and the background rate of appendicitis is too high for this to be considered a safety concern that requires any specific assessments and can be addressed by routine post-marketing surveillance.
L1138: There were no organ-systems for which SAEs occurred at a higher frequency in the naldemedine group, with the exception of hepatobiliary disorders (4 in the naldemedine group vs. 1 in the placebo group), and these events did not appear to be treatment-related.
L1139: Of the other non-GI-related SAE preferred terms, this reviewer identified 3 notable types of SAEs that occurred more frequently in the naldemedine group, though the overall rate of all of these events were low (&lt;0.5%):
L1140: - Myocardial infarction (4 in the naldemedine arm vs. 1 in the placebo arm)
- Pulmonary embolism (2 in the naldemedine group vs. 0 in the placebo arm)
- Overdoses (4 subjects in the naldemedine group vs. 2 in the placebo arm)
L1141: Myocardial infarction and pulmonary embolism were considered notable due to their relevance to MACE assessments. Although a treatment difference was observed based on AE preferred terms, the number of myocardial infarctions based on CAC adjudication was the same in both groups (3 events per arm), though one of the 4 events in the naldemedine arm with the assigned the preferred AE term “myocardial infarction” was not adjudicated by the CAC. Another of these myocardial infarction SAEs was fatal (Subject 52449-005 in the naldemedine arm), and this event was classified by the CAC as “cardiovascular death.” In the placebo arm, one event of “angina unstable” was classified as a myocardial infarction by CAC adjudication, and 2 SAEs were classified as cardiovascular death (Subject 52442-017 with “arteriolosclerosis” and 52437-005 with “cardiac arrest”). Also, one SAE in each arm was classified as a myocardial infarction, type 2, by the CAC (i.e., myocardial ischemia secondary to another event—Subject 12322-008 with “supraventricular tachycardia” and 22341-011 with “syncope and hypotension.” These events are discussed in more detail in Section 8.5.1.
L1142: Overdoses represent an event of interest due as a potential risk if opioid dosages are acutely increased due to decreased opioid efficacy by naldemedine. As noted above, 6 subjects with on-treatment SAEs of overdose involving opioid medication(s) were reported (4 in the naldemedine arm and 2 in the placebo arm). In addition, this reviewer identified 6 additional cases (3 in each group) that are consistent with either definite or possible opioid overdose through a search of narratives coded under related AE terms (e.g., toxic encephalopathy, metabolic encephalopathy, mental status change, altered level of consciousness, and respiratory depression) and for subjects treated with naloxone. During the review of these cases, a high degree of overall morbidity, concomitant mental illnesses, and polypharmacy was observed across subjects in both arms, and no treatment difference could be identified. Narratives for these 12 subjects (7 naldemedine, 5 placebo) are provided in Table 72.
L1143: This reviewer also identified the following 16 SAEs of interest, based upon overall TEAE data, possible mechanism, or previously identified safety concerns either for this drug or the PAMORA drug class. Narratives and reviewer comments for these events are provided in Table 71.
L1144: - Subject 10602-002: Allergic reaction/hypersensitivity (possible anaphylaxis; discussion in Section 8.4.4)
- Subject 12302-011: Premature ventricular contractions in triplets
- Subject 12322-008: Supraventricular tachycardia
- Subject 12333-005: COPD exacerbation
- Subject 50409-006: Congestive heart failure (2 events)
- Subject 50709-005: Sinus tachycardia
- Subject 52325-016: Hepatitis (discussed in section 9.4.7, Liver Events)
- Subject 52331-005: Stress
- Subject 52334-017: Peripheral arterial occlusive disease, skin necrosis
- Subject 52349-008: Femoral artery occlusion
L1145: - Subject 52362-021: Sickle cell anemia with crisis
- Subject 52415-004: Pulmonary embolism, deep venous, thromobosis, myocardial infarction, adenocarcinoma
- Subject 52428-007: Pulmonary embolism, pneumonia
L1146: One subject (12305-005) with patent ductus arteriosus underwent cardiac catheterization for PDA closure on Study Day 21. Although this procedure was not planned at the time of screening, medical records from the treating cardiologist specifically indicate that the subject experienced no worsening in this pre-existing condition after initiation of study treatment.

52216-005	Abdominal Pain	50/F	1	7	Moderate	Withdrawn	Resolved
The subject experienced sudden onset of epigastric pain, associated with nausea and vomiting few hours after the first dose study drug (Day 1) and was hospitalized from Study Day 2 to 7 with resolution of symptoms. The subject was on an established laxative regimen at baseline.
Notable findings on evaluation include BP 147/93, pulse 95, and ALT 204 U/L; Abdominal CT showed possible wall thickening of sigmoid colon.
Treatment included metronidazole for presumed colitis, as well as duloxetine, cyclizine, acetylsalicylic acid, pregabalin, morphine, and prucalopride.
The study drug was discontinued on Day 1 and subject was withdrawn from the study on the same day due to this SAE
Reviewer comment: This event is consistent with a treatment-related GI reaction and is supported by the rapid onset of symptoms following administration of the first dose. In addition, the patient's symptoms (abdominal pain, nausea, and vomiting) are typical of naldemedine GI toxicity, which has not been associated with demonstrable tissue injury and resolve without sequelae following treatment discontinuation, as observed with this subject. The changes in the sigmoid colon seen on CT can be attributed to chronic constipation, and a diagnosis of colitis is less likely.
Of note, the elevated ALT level reported with the SAE was not included among study data, and this subject was not flagged as a patient with transaminase elevation. No follow-up data are available. Therefore, a treatment-related transaminitis cannot be ruled out.

10602-002	Allergic Reaction	55/F	1	1	Moderate	Withdrawn	Resolved
Onset of hypersensitivity reaction within 2 hours after administration of 1st dose
Symptoms of diarrhea and vomiting, followed by bronchospasm and dyspnea (treated in ER due to respiratory symptoms and persistence of GI symptoms)
Elevated BP (177/96) and HR (106), no hypotension or hypoxia reported
Evaluation notable for bilateral wheezing and leukocytosis (20.1/mm3); also reported slight elevations of CRP, transaminases, and glucose (no results)
Prior medical history includes GI conditions of diverticulosis, hiatal hernia, and gastric ulcer disease; respiratory conditions of asthma, pneumonia, and pulmonary embolism; allergies to penicillin, doxycycline, tetracycline, iodine, benzododecinium bromide (topical antiseptic), and adhesive, with history of anaphylactic shock to general anesthesia; environmental allergies (pollen, dust mites, bee stings)
Treatment with promethazine, omeprazole, and metoclopramide for vomiting; and fenoterol and anbroxol for wheezing
Reviewer comment: This SAE is consistent with a treatment-related hypersensitivity reaction that meets clinical criteria for anaphylaxis due to the concurrent (1) presence of respiratory compromise and (2) persistent gastrointestinal symptoms, occurring rapidly after exposure (minutes to several hours) [Sampson 2006]. While the subject's GI symptoms are consistent with other nalmedine GI reactions, and vomiting could have triggered bronchospasm, the co-occurrence of bronchospasm is suggestive of a hypersensitivity reaction. The subject's medical history would support either scenario, and this reviewer notes that treatment with antihistamines or corticosteroids were not reported. Therefore, in absence of cutaneous manifestations or hypotension, this reviewer considers this SAE to be a likely hypersensitivity reaction, possibly anaphylaxis.

Since opioids, particularly morphine used for treatment of CHF due to their vasodilatory effects [REF] and is currently the subject of academic studies, including a large scale clinical trial comparing morphine and fentanyl in acute pulmonary edema, the potential effects of opioid antagonism needs to be considered.

L1175: ## Post-treatment, treatment-emergent SAEs
L1176: Although delayed treatment-related adverse effects would not be expected with after discontinuation of naldemedine and has not been reported with this drug class, the long-term effects of these drugs are not fully known. Overall, a review of TEAEs occurring &gt;5 days after treatment discontinuation, as well as AEs with onset after Day 14 of the follow-up period, did not identify any new safety concerns. However, the following 3 treatment-emergent SAEs in the
L1177: naldemedine arm with onset &gt;5 days after discontinuation are considered events of interest based on the safety concerns for this medication class.
L1178: - **12333-025: Deep venous thrombosis (moderate, resolving)**- 58 year-old female experienced an SAE deep vein thrombosis (DVT) of the right leg with acute onset of symptoms on Day 23 of the 28-day follow-up period (Study Day 107).
- **12351-008: Transient ischemic attack (mild, resolved)**- 74 year-old female with history of seizure disorder and prior gunshot to the frontal lobe experienced an SAE of transient ischemic attack (TIA) on Day 14 of the 28-day follow-up period (Study Day 102). The subject reported feeling disoriented, agitated, and confused while at an orthopedic visit. Symptoms reported during hospitalization included headache, memory loss, dizziness, lightheadedness, blurred vision, general weakness, and a raspy voice. On evaluation, the subject was awake, alert, and oriented; vital signs were notable for a BP of 160/70 mmHg, and ECG showed right bundle branch block and nonspecific ST changes. Head CT showed no obvious mass or hemorrhage, but the study was limited due to extreme artifact due to left frontal cranioplasty and focal metallic fragment in the right frontal lobe. Similarly, CT angiogram of head and neck showed no significant arterial stenosis, vascular occlusion, thrombosis, or aneurysm, but was limited by artifact. The consulting neurologist considered the event to be consistent with a possible complex headache. Symptoms improved within 24 hours, and the subject was discharged from the hospital on continued treatment with carbamazepine. The event was adjudicated as a non-cardiovascular event by the CAC.
- **Subject 22321-018: ischemic colitis (moderate, resolved)**- 56 year-old female was admitted to the hospital on Day 61 (29 days after study drug discontinuation) with diagnosis of possible ischemic colitis. The subject presented with 'stringy diarrhea with blood', associated with sharp, stabbing left lower quadrant abdominal pain, following passage of hard stool. Biopsy of right colon and rectum was normal. Colonoscopy revealed acute mild to moderate inflammation located 25 to 35 cm from the anal verge and appeared related to ischemic colitis or infectious colitis, and CT of the abdomen showed significant decompression of the sigmoid colon.
L1179: The study drug was discontinued on Study Day 32 due to a treatment-emergent SAE of lymphadenopathy. The subject presented at that time with tachycardia, sweating, and flushing. CT scan showed multiple and hilar lymph nodes were also noted multiple pulmonary nodules/lesions; and thoracic, mediastinal, and hilar lymphadenopathy. It was reported that differential diagnoses of cancer, sarcoidosis, lymphoma, and metastatic lung cancer were all ruled out.
L1180: *The event of "ischemic colitis" occurred in the context of OIC after study drug discontinuation and was reported to be a diagnosis of exclusion. There was no evidence of vascular compromise.*
L1181: ## 8.4.3. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Events
L1182: In the pooled phase 3 safety population, 75 subjects in the naldemedine group had a premature study termination attributed to a TEAE (6.4%), vs. 53 subjects in the placebo group (4.5%). One additional subject in the naldemedine withdrew due to an AE identified prior to study drug administration (i.e., not a TEAE). AEs leading to study discontinuation are summarized in Table 72.
L1183: The AEs leading to early study termination have been divided into subgroups of GI AE, events of opioid withdrawal (OW), pain-related AE, and “other” AEs that do not fit into one of the other categories. Subjects with both OW and GI events were categorized only in the OW group. Pain-related events only included those whose verbatim terms directly cited increased pain or reduced opioid efficacy as the cause of discontinuation. Consistent with the overall safety profile of this drug, GI AEs were largest subgroup of discontinuations among naldemedine-treated subjects. In the placebo arm, the “other” subgroup was the largest group of AEs leading to study discontinuation. This difference between treatment groups supports a causal relationship between GI events and study treatment. In the pooled Phase 3 safety population, 3.2% of naldemedine-treated subjects withdrew enrollment due to GI events, compared to 1.5% of placebo-treated subjects. Within the subgroup of GI events, the 4 main GI symptoms of naldemedine toxicity (abdominal pain, diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting) accounted for all GI-related study discontinuations in the naldemedine arm (n=37), vs. 13 of 17 GI-related discontinuations in the placebo arm. GI reactions due to naldemedine treatment are discussed in detail in Section 8.4.4.

L1191: ## Hypersensitivity
L1192: There are 3 total subjects in the pooled phase 3 safety population with events of interest related to hypersensitivity (all in the naldemedine arm).
L1193: Two subjects with study discontinuation due to events consistent with hypersensitivity (Subject 10602-001 with an SAE meeting criteria for anaphylaxis [9] and Subject 52369-012). Both of these subjects experienced gastrointestinal symptoms characteristic of naldemedine GI toxicity.
L1194: - Subject 10602-002 (full narrative in Section 8.4.2): SAE of “allergic reaction” with onset of abdominal pain, diarrhea, vomiting, and bronchospasm within 2 hours of administration of the first dose of naldemedine. Based on Sampson criteria [9], this event meets criteria for possible anaphylaxis due to co-occurrence of respiratory distress and persistent gastrointestinal symptoms shortly after exposure to a potential allergen. The subject received treatment for this SAE in the emergency department, including antiemetics (promethazine, metoclopramide, and thiethylpromazine), inhaled beta 2-adrenergic agonist (fenoterol), omeprazole, and ambroxol (mucolytic). The subject recovered from the event on the same day. Study treatment was discontinued on Day 1 due to this event.
L1195: (Although this event meets diagnostic criteria for possible anaphylaxis, this reviewer notes that treatment of the event did not include antihistamines or corticosteroids. In addition, the subject did not experience hypotension or any cutaneous manifestations of hypersensitivity. On the contrary, the subject's blood pressures were elevated during the event.)
L1196: - Subject 52369-012: AEs of abdominal pain/cramps, diarrhea, and rash on chest with onset within a couple of hours of the first dose of naldemedine. The subject discontinued study participation on Day 7 due to persistent symptoms. No treatment was administered for the AE.
L1197: (This event may represent a hypersensitivity reaction but does not meet criteria for anaphylaxis.[9])
L1198: One additional subject had a TEAE of rash (onset Study Day 2), leading to study discontinuation. This event of rash without associated symptoms could be considered a possible hypersensitivity reaction based on temporal relationship to study treatment.
L1199: - Subject 52426-013: 47-year-old female with medical history of hypersensitivity and eczema who developed a rash (left armpit) starting on Study Day 2 and rash (right armpit, moderate severity) starting on Study Day 6. Study treatment was discontinued on Day 6. The first occurrence of rash (left armpit, moderate severity) resolved on Day 10, and the second occurrence of rash (left armpit) resolved on Day 20. No treatment was provided for the rash. No other AEs were reported for this subject.
L1200: ## 8.4.4. Significant Adverse Events
L1201: ## Summary of Significant Adverse Events
L1202: Gastrointestinal drug toxicity, presenting most commonly with symptoms of abdominal pain, diarrhea, nausea, and/or vomiting, was the most frequent type of adverse reaction in naldemedine clinical trials (i.e., adverse events reasonably attributable to study drug treatment). In the judgment of this reviewer, GI adverse reactions are the most significant contributor to the overall safety profile of this drug due, in part, to the disproportionate number of study discontinuations and/or treatment interruptions attributed to these reactions. Nevertheless, serious GI reactions were uncommon, and symptoms typically resolved rapidly upon treatment discontinuation or resolved spontaneously in spite of ongoing treatment. None of the treatment-emergent GI events were considered reasonably likely to be due to study drug treatment were associated with life-threatening complications or long-term sequelae. [The reader is referred to the Subsection below, entitled “Gastrointestinal (GI) Drug Reactions/Toxicity” for a more detailed review of GI safety data.]
L1203: Opioid withdrawal represented the other major group of naldemedine adverse reactions, with events (including possible cases-see definitions). Similar to GI reactions, opioid withdrawal events were non-life threatening, but are considered notable for their contribution to study discontinuations. The rate of opioid withdrawal was low' no change to recommendations regarding doses is needed.
L1204: ## Gastrointestinal (GI) Drug Reactions/Toxicity
L1205: The most common presenting symptoms of naldemedine GI toxicity were abdominal pain, diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting. In controlled phase 3 studies, 20.9% of naldemedine-treated subjects experienced \ge 1 of these 4 GI symptoms during study treatment (vs. of placebo-treated patients)—12-week studies and 52-week trial.
L1206: ## Abdominal pain and diarrhea
L1207: Although this is not a comprehensive list of AE terms corresponding to naldemedine ARs, and these symptoms also frequently with events not attributable to naldemedine treatment, this group of terms captures the majority of subjects who experienced naldemedine GI toxicity.
L1208: Though plausible and are described due to the risk of gastrointestinal perforation with PAMORA, for which cases have only been reported with subcutaneous Relistor but are considered a class-wide risk.
L1209: One distinguishing feature of GI toxicity is the generally early onset of symptoms, compared to
L1210: GI events in the placebo arm which were more dispersed throughout study duration.
L1211: Of the naldemedine subjects who reported \ge 1 GI AE, 51.5% reported onset of symptom(s) in the first week of treatment (29.7% on Study Day 1) during Week 1. By comparison, 26.1%, of all subjects in the placebo arm with \ge 1 GI AE had onset of symptoms during the first week of study treatment (5.9% on Day 1).
L1212: Figure 30 and Figure 31 show the distribution of AE onset for the GI AEs of abdominal pain, diarrhea, nausea, and vomiting for naldemedine-treated subjects in the 12-week trials (Studies V9231 and V9232) and Study V9235, respectively (subject's first episode only).

L1217: A major consideration during this review was the evaluation of naldemedine safety with concurrent laxative use. It is important to consider that ~2/3 of the V9235 study population was on a stable, established laxative at baseline that was continued into the treatment period when interpreting the relative frequencies of GI events.
L1218: A higher incidence of GI AEs was observed in the first 12 weeks of Study V9235, compared to Studies V9231 and V9232. However, it is difficult to attribute the higher rate of events to concomitant use of laxatives. In naldemedine-treated subject, the proportions of subjects with GI events and study discontinuations due to AEs were similar between subjects with and without baseline laxative regimens. This most notable difference was a higher rate of GI events in placebo subjects on baseline laxatives, compared to those without baseline laxative use.
